62 [SOURCE: Page_62]

Chapitre 3. Le bricolage comme innovation : Ouvrir la boîte noire du système [SOURCE: Page_63]

d’irrigation au goutte à goutte [SOURCE: Page_63]

Ce chapitre a été publié sous forme d’article : [SOURCE: Page_63]

Benouniche, M., Zwarteveen, M., & Kuper, M. (2014). Bricolage as innovation: opening the black box of drip irrigation systems. Irrigation and Drainage. DOI: [SOURCE: Page_63]

10.1002/ird.1854 [SOURCE: Page_63]

L’irrigation au goutte à goutte connait un développement rapide de par le monde, [SOURCE: Page_63]

engendrant une multiplicité de matériels et de systèmes d’irrigation, en particulier à [SOURCE: Page_63]

partir des années 1970. Un manuel de la FAO publié en 1983 (Vermeiren et Jobling, [SOURCE: Page_63]

1983) a même affirmé que la diversité des équipements était si grande, et les modèles [SOURCE: Page_63]

changeaient si rapidement, qu'il était impossible de les énumérer et de les décrire. Ceci a [SOURCE: Page_63]

incité différentes institutions concernées par l’irrigation du monde entier à développer [SOURCE: Page_63]

des standards pour l’irrigation au goutte à goutte (Dedrick 1986; Solomon and Dedrick, [SOURCE: Page_63]

1995). Ces standards ont été progressivement centralisés dans des institutions comme [SOURCE: Page_63]

le Comité européen de normalisation (CEN), et l'Organisation internationale de [SOURCE: Page_63]

normalisation (ISO). La mise en place des standards reflète le souhait des ingénieurs à [SOURCE: Page_63]

classer et de à distinguer la technique entre « bonne » et « mauvaise » technologies, une [SOURCE: Page_63]

distinction qui fonctionne simultanément à protéger leur savoir-­‐faire. En effet, à travers [SOURCE: Page_63]

cette standardisation l’ingénieur ambitionne de se rendre maitre de la technique pour [SOURCE: Page_63]

détenir l’unique savoir concernant la technologie. Au Maroc, le goutte à goutte a été [SOURCE: Page_63]

introduit dans les grandes exploitations agricoles dans les années 1970 et 1980. [SOURCE: Page_63]

L'utilisation de systèmes d'irrigation goutte à goutte basés sur les normes [SOURCE: Page_63]

internationales rencontraient des problèmes en raison leur coût, mais aussi à cause de [SOURCE: Page_63]

problèmes techniques (par exemple le colmatage des goutteurs), et le manque [SOURCE: Page_63]

d'expérience des utilisateurs (Popp, 1984). Dans ce chapitre, nous montrons, comment [SOURCE: Page_63]

les acteurs non-­‐conventionnels à travers un processus qu’ils qualifient de bricolage ont [SOURCE: Page_63]

adapté les installations, et créé des normes locales et remettent en cause les normes [SOURCE: Page_63]

internationales. Dans ce chapitre, nous documentons trois instances de bricolage : 1) la [SOURCE: Page_63]

première instance concernait l’ensemble des acteurs locaux occupé à faire marcher des [SOURCE: Page_63]

équipements importés à travers un bricolage au quotidien, 2) la deuxième instance était [SOURCE: Page_63]

relative à une réingénierie des systèmes d'irrigation au goutte à goutte pour les adapter [SOURCE: Page_63]

aux conditions locales, et 3) pendant la troisième instance, les différentes composantes [SOURCE: Page_63]

adaptées intégraient les normes nationales afin de se qualifier pour la procédure de [SOURCE: Page_63]

subventions de l'Etat. Le bricolage a ainsi permis la conception de systèmes plus adaptés [SOURCE: Page_63]

aux conditions locales, mais aussi un apprentissage graduel de la technique. Dans ce [SOURCE: Page_63]

chapitre, nous postulons que la système d’irrigation au goutte à goutte n’est pas une [SOURCE: Page_63]

boite fermée où seuls les ingénieurs détiennent la clé. Elle nécessite parfois des [SOURCE: Page_63]

modifications selon les conditions d’utilisations pour qu’elle fonctionne. Nous montrons [SOURCE: Page_63]

ainsi que la technique d’irrigation au goutte a connu un succès important auprès de ses [SOURCE: Page_63]

63 [SOURCE: Page_63]

utilisateurs précisément par ce qu’elle se prête au bricolage. A travers ce processus les [SOURCE: Page_64]

acteurs locaux ont ainsi pris le contrôle de l’innovation et prennent des responsabilités [SOURCE: Page_64]

dans des domaines que l’on pensait réservés aux ingénieurs. [SOURCE: Page_64]

Notre étude contribue à enrichir les débats scientifiques à deux niveaux ; le premier [SOURCE: Page_64]

dans les sciences d’innovation, où nous démontrons que le processus d’innovation n’est [SOURCE: Page_64]

pas linéaire mais un processus complexe, largement imprévisible et créatif qui consiste [SOURCE: Page_64]

en interactions et négociations itératives entre agriculteurs, ingénieurs et de nombreux [SOURCE: Page_64]

autres acteurs. Ce processus peut ainsi conduire à de nouvelles normes, dynamiques et [SOURCE: Page_64]

localisées pour la technologie et où la notion de bricolage reconnaît ainsi que les [SOURCE: Page_64]

technologies ont toujours besoin de modifications et d'adaptations pour les rendre [SOURCE: Page_64]

utilisables dans des contextes spécifiques de différents utilisateurs et différents usages [SOURCE: Page_64]

pour en faire un goutte à goutte « de proximité ». Deuxièmement, notre étude remet en [SOURCE: Page_64]

cause la place de l’ingénieur dans le monde du goutte à goutte. Aujourd’hui cet ingénieur [SOURCE: Page_64]

doit accepter qu’il n’est plus le détenteur unique de la technologie, il se trouve obligé [SOURCE: Page_64]

d’intégrer le processus de bricolage pour pouvoir continuer sa pratique professionnelle. [SOURCE: Page_64]

64 [SOURCE: Page_64]

## MAYA BENOUNICHE4,5, MARGREET ZWARTEVEEN6 AND MARCEL KUPER4,5

In Morocco, many farmers enthusiastically use drip irrigation. However, few drip [SOURCE: Page_65]

irrigation systems conform to engineering standards. In a process they refer to as [SOURCE: Page_65]

bricolage, farmers modify and adapt standard designs, thus creating their own technical [SOURCE: Page_65]

standards. We document three instances of bricolage and show that it is a useful term to [SOURCE: Page_65]

explain irrigation innovation processes. Through bricolage farmers adapt and modify [SOURCE: Page_65]

the system to their needs, but also enter a process of gradual learning about what drip [SOURCE: Page_65]

irrigation is and what it can achieve. Bricolage has led to the multiplication and [SOURCE: Page_65]

diversification of drip systems, with different categories of users co-­‐designing the nature [SOURCE: Page_65]

and direction of change it provokes. Through bricolage, local actors effectively share [SOURCE: Page_65]

responsibilities of the design process with engineers. The paper concludes that the fact [SOURCE: Page_65]

that drip irrigation lends itself to bricolage helps explain its success as an innovation. [SOURCE: Page_65]

Key words: drip irrigation; innovation; adaptation; learning; standards; Morocco [SOURCE: Page_65]

Received 4 June 2013; Revised 28 January 2014; Accepted 28 January 2014 [SOURCE: Page_65]

Au Maroc, de nombreux agriculteurs utilisent l’irrigation au goutte à goutte. Pourtant, [SOURCE: Page_65]

peu de systèmes de goutte à goutte sont conformes aux normes fixées par des [SOURCE: Page_65]

installations, et créent des normes locales. Nous documentons trois instances de [SOURCE: Page_65]

bricolage, et montrons que le terme bricolage est utile pour expliquer le processus [SOURCE: Page_65]

d’innovation en irrigation. Le bricolage a permis la conception de systèmes plus adaptés [SOURCE: Page_65]

aux conditions locales, mais permet aussi un apprentissage graduel de la technique, et [SOURCE: Page_65]

plus généralement, d’apprivoiser le changement. Les acteurs locaux ont ainsi pris le [SOURCE: Page_65]

contrôle de l’innovation et prennent des responsabilités dans des domaines que l’on [SOURCE: Page_65]

pensait réservés aux ingénieurs. [SOURCE: Page_65]

## INTRODUCTION

The rapid worldwide development of drip irrigation resulted in a multiplication of [SOURCE: Page_66]

irrigation equipment, especially from the 1970s onwards. A manual of the Food and [SOURCE: Page_66]

Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) published in 1983 (Vermeiren and [SOURCE: Page_66]

Jobling, 1983) even stated that the variety of equipment was so great, and the models [SOURCE: Page_66]

changed so rapidly, that it was impossible to enumerate and describe them. This [SOURCE: Page_66]

prompted different institutions around the world to develop standards for drip [SOURCE: Page_66]

irrigation from (Dedrick, 1986; Solomon andDedrick, 1995). These standardswere [SOURCE: Page_66]

gradually centralized in institutions like the European Committee for Standardization [SOURCE: Page_66]

(CEN), and the International Organization for Standardization (ISO). Standardization [SOURCE: Page_66]

reflects the wish of engineers to allow us to distinguish between ’good’ and ’bad’ [SOURCE: Page_66]

technologies, a distinction that simultaneously works to protect their expertise. In this [SOURCE: Page_66]

article, we describe field experiences with drip irrigation in Morocco to call into [SOURCE: Page_66]

question the desirability and possibility of universal standards for irrigation technology. [SOURCE: Page_66]

Drip irrigation was introduced in Morocco on large-­‐scale farms in the 1970s, following [SOURCE: Page_66]

exchanges with international scientists and engineers. Tabet (1972) mentions a lecture [SOURCE: Page_66]

on drip irrigation in Morocco of an irrigation scientist from the University of California [SOURCE: Page_66]

in 1972, as well as exchanges concerning drip irrigation during the 1972 conference in [SOURCE: Page_66]

Varna of the International Commission on Irrigation and Drainage (ICID). On large-­‐scale [SOURCE: Page_66]

farms, the use of drip irrigation systems based on international standards encountered [SOURCE: Page_66]

problems due to their cost, but also because of technical problems (e.g. clogging of [SOURCE: Page_66]

drippers), and the lack of experience of users (Popp, 1984). Small-­‐scale farmers [SOURCE: Page_66]

introduced drip irrigation from the mid-­‐ 1990s onwards, and even more acutely faced [SOURCE: Page_66]

such problems (Poncet, 2010). State subsidies were progressively introduced to [SOURCE: Page_66]

promote drip irrigation, which accelerated the spread of the system to even more users, [SOURCE: Page_66]

especially when subsidy rates increased after 2006 (Benouniche et al., 2011). [SOURCE: Page_66]

In this paper, we postulate that the rapid spread of drip irrigation to a wide range of [SOURCE: Page_66]

farmers inMorocco can be explained by the fact that farmers and local craftsmen opened [SOURCE: Page_66]

the black box of drip irrigation systems, adapting it to their own situation, through a [SOURCE: Page_66]

process that we refer to as bricolage. This is a term often used by local actors when [SOURCE: Page_66]

referring to drip irrigation, but it also resonates well with recent works by innovation [SOURCE: Page_66]

scholars, who define innovation as a process of adaptation and appropriation (Alter, [SOURCE: Page_66]

2002). Garud and Karnøe (2003) used the term in a study on contrasting technological [SOURCE: Page_66]

innovation in windmills in Denmark and the United States, showing how a bricolage [SOURCE: Page_66]

approach that ’begins with a low-­‐tech design but ramps up progressively’ is able to [SOURCE: Page_66]

prevail over a high-­‐tech breakthrough approach. Through bricolage, different categories [SOURCE: Page_66]

of users appropriate the technology, co-­‐steering the nature and direction of change it [SOURCE: Page_66]

takes. Bricolage can thus be seen as a role reversal, with farmers co-­‐steering the design [SOURCE: Page_66]

process with engineers. In this study, we consider bricolage as a creative process of [SOURCE: Page_66]

learning and adaptation—a process which changes both the users’ context and the drip [SOURCE: Page_66]

irrigation technology itself (and its meaning). [SOURCE: Page_66]

66 [SOURCE: Page_66]

This paper documents three specific instances of bricolage on drip irrigation systems in [SOURCE: Page_67]

Morocco. Our hypothesis is that bricolage enables farmers, and local craftsmen, to take [SOURCE: Page_67]

control of the different functions of innovation (production, distribution, utilization; [SOURCE: Page_67]

Geels, 2004), producing multiple drip irrigation systems for multiple purposes. [SOURCE: Page_67]

Acknowledging bricolage, and the multiplication of systems it produces, thus calls into [SOURCE: Page_67]

question uniform design standards and distributes engineering agency beyond the [SOURCE: Page_67]

engineering community. [SOURCE: Page_67]

## METHODOLOGY AND STUDY AREA

The study area is located in the Saiss Plain, a rich agricultural area between the cities of [SOURCE: Page_67]

Meknes and Fes in northwest Morocco. In the past, the farming systems were mainly [SOURCE: Page_67]

rain-­‐fed (cereals, vineyards), with some irrigated crops (tobacco, orchards, horticulture) [SOURCE: Page_67]

in some small surface irrigation schemes. Following droughts in the 1980s, many [SOURCE: Page_67]

farmers progressively turned to groundwater use, which led to a sharp increase in the [SOURCE: Page_67]

area under orchards (42% of the irrigated area) and horticulture (35%). The river basin [SOURCE: Page_67]

agency estimates the current irrigated area to be 37 000 ha, of which 25 000 ha depend [SOURCE: Page_67]

on groundwater through 12 000 individual (tube) wells. Half of the irrigated area is [SOURCE: Page_67]

taken up by drip irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_67]

We interviewed the actors involved in designing and implementing drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_67]

systems in the official subsidy procedure (two official firms, two representatives from [SOURCE: Page_67]

the agricultural administration). Using a checklist (date of installation, procedure of [SOURCE: Page_67]

design, operation and maintenance (O&M)), and observing their drip irrigation systems [SOURCE: Page_67]

(type of system, components, state of the equipment, adaptations), we then interviewed [SOURCE: Page_67]

30 farmers who had installed drip irrigation in the past 25 years. We categorized these [SOURCE: Page_67]

farmers on the basis of the logic underlying their use of drip irrigation, as this [SOURCE: Page_67]

determines the type of system farmers install. We identified four groups: (i) notables [SOURCE: Page_67]

(5); these farmers first introduced drip irrigation in their large-­‐scale farms (>100 ha); [SOURCE: Page_67]

(ii) medium-­‐scale reference farmers (3) aim for excellence in agricultural production on [SOURCE: Page_67]

their farms (10–15 ha); these farmers are a reference for other farmers, hence their [SOURCE: Page_67]

name; (iii) small-­‐scale innovators (3) were the first to introduce drip irrigation on small-­‐ scale farms (<5 ha), and later facilitated the diffusion of drip irrigation to other small-­‐ scale farmers; (iv) small horticultural farmers (19) represent the majority of small-­‐scale [SOURCE: Page_67]

farmers (<5ha) using drip irrigation; they have a pragmatic view of what drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_67]

can do, ranging from water and/or labour saving to the precise application of inputs to [SOURCE: Page_67]

achieve better yields. When farmers did not irrigate their land themselves, we [SOURCE: Page_67]

interviewed their farm managers and labourers. Since only a minority of farmers had [SOURCE: Page_67]

applied for a subsidy, and most farmers referred to informal actors involved in designing [SOURCE: Page_67]

and installing drip irrigation systems, we then interviewed these actors. We interviewed [SOURCE: Page_67]

four local fitters, three retailers and six ’welders’ who manufacture and repair drip [SOURCE: Page_67]

irrigation systems. In interviews with farmers and other actors we did not use the term [SOURCE: Page_67]

bricolage, employing more neutral terms (changes, adaptations). [SOURCE: Page_67]

67 [SOURCE: Page_67]

However, all actors referred to bricolage, a Frenchword assimilated into the local Arabic [SOURCE: Page_68]

dialect of darija and employed unchanged from the French. Subsequently, we checked [SOURCE: Page_68]

with our interviewees what they meant by this term, and what it represented to them as [SOURCE: Page_68]

a practice. [SOURCE: Page_68]

## RESULTS

The process of bricolage on drip irrigation in the Saiss [SOURCE: Page_68]

We identified three instances of bricolage related to the diffusion of drip irrigation in the [SOURCE: Page_68]

Saiss (see Figure 1). The first instance corresponded to a process of learning by local [SOURCE: Page_68]

actors on how to work and maintain imported equipment; the second related to a [SOURCE: Page_68]

process of adaptation of the technology involving the re-­‐engineering of drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_68]

systems; the third corresponded to a process of adaptation of local drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_68]

systems to meet the standards of official subsidy procedures. The procedures applied in [SOURCE: Page_68]

the field were adapted to accommodate locally engineered systems with locally [SOURCE: Page_68]

produced parts. [SOURCE: Page_68]

Figure 1. Three instances of bricolage which explain the diffusion of drip irrigation in [SOURCE: Page_68]

the Saiss. [SOURCE: Page_68]

68 [SOURCE: Page_68]

Instance 1: Making it work, day-­‐to-­‐day bricolage on imported equipment [SOURCE: Page_69]

From the mid-­‐1980s onwards, companies in Casablanca who imported international [SOURCE: Page_69]

standard equipment introduced drip irrigation on large-­‐scale farms, belonging to the [SOURCE: Page_69]

notables in the Saiss on a trial basis, often free of charge. Farm labourers helped install [SOURCE: Page_69]

drip irrigation systems (digging the trenches, installing main and secondary lines) and [SOURCE: Page_69]

thus discovered a new technique. They discussed with the engineers and technicians [SOURCE: Page_69]

who came to install the systems, and observed. They were curious about the new [SOURCE: Page_69]

technique, the different components of the system, and their use. [SOURCE: Page_69]

Managers and labourers had to operate the new systems, usually with very limited [SOURCE: Page_69]

training. Few fellow farmers had any experience in operating the systems, and a local [SOURCE: Page_69]

support sector had not yet emerged. The companies that had fitted the systems were [SOURCE: Page_69]

more than 200 km away. Hence, local users of drip irrigation had to make-­‐do, relying on [SOURCE: Page_69]

their own ingenuity: [SOURCE: Page_69]

’I was in charge of the labourers operating the drip irrigation system. Whenever there was [SOURCE: Page_69]

a problem, I tried to find quick solutions, especially when the emitters clogged up. I didn’t [SOURCE: Page_69]

tell the farm manager every time there was a problem, because that would mean [SOURCE: Page_69]

contacting the company in Casablanca. The company would take time to deal with the [SOURCE: Page_69]

problem, and we would lose a few days of irrigation. I had to manage, and so I used [SOURCE: Page_69]

bricolage on the main lines, the drip tubing, and the filter systems…’ (Mohamed, labourer, [SOURCE: Page_69]

aged 31) [SOURCE: Page_69]

Through bricolage, labourers and farm managers learned how to operate and maintain [SOURCE: Page_69]

drip irrigation systems. However, this did not lead to the disappearance of bricolage. [SOURCE: Page_69]

They had come to appreciate bricolage as an almost regular feature of operating drip [SOURCE: Page_69]

systems. [SOURCE: Page_69]

Instance 2: Re-­‐engineering drip irrigation systems to adapt them to local conditions [SOURCE: Page_69]

In the second instance of bricolage farmers went beyond operation and maintenance to [SOURCE: Page_69]

start to physically modify their systems. Farm managers and labourers on the farms of [SOURCE: Page_69]

the notables gradually adapted the system to their personal situation (knowledge, [SOURCE: Page_69]

training) and to the farm structure (cropping systems, topography, who is irrigating). [SOURCE: Page_69]

They removed certain components, and replaced others that regularly broke down, or [SOURCE: Page_69]

did not function satisfactorily: [SOURCE: Page_69]

’when the company fitted the automatic system, I thought it would be easy to manage. [SOURCE: Page_69]

Everything was programmed. The engineer had explained when and how to start [SOURCE: Page_69]

irrigating. After a week, I noticed we were under-­‐irrigating the crops. I contacted the [SOURCE: Page_69]

company, but nobody came. We had to find a solution, as we had already lost some days of [SOURCE: Page_69]

irrigation. I talked to my boss and suggested replacing the electric valves with manual ones [SOURCE: Page_69]

so we could operate the system more easily, and replacing the automatic fertiliser [SOURCE: Page_69]

dispenser by smaller manual ones to measure the quantity of the fertilizer more precisely’. [SOURCE: Page_69]

(Driss, farm manager, aged 42) [SOURCE: Page_69]

69 [SOURCE: Page_69]

Some medium-­‐scale farmers had observed the advantages of drip irrigation (higher [SOURCE: Page_70]

yields, better quality products), and installed it on their farms. These reference farmers, [SOURCE: Page_70]

who were aiming for excellence, often had problems with the filter systems, which were [SOURCE: Page_70]

not suited to local conditions, or to their farming practices. This was especially the case [SOURCE: Page_70]

in the early days when irrigation companies did not have a large choice of equipment, [SOURCE: Page_70]

and only offered standard solutions: [SOURCE: Page_70]

’In 1997, after one year of use, problems started. The tubing got clogged every time; I didn’t [SOURCE: Page_70]

understand why as I had a strainer filter. I asked the advice of a retailer nearby, who [SOURCE: Page_70]

suggested I replace it by a disk filter. I didn’t want to bother to go all the way to [SOURCE: Page_70]

Casablanca, so I replaced it myself.’ (Mostafa, farmer, aged 40) [SOURCE: Page_70]

He refers to the process by which he replaced the filter system as bricolage. Making use [SOURCE: Page_70]

of the emerging local support sector (advisers, fitters, retailers, welders), he [SOURCE: Page_70]

personalized his drip irrigation system, according to his needs. [SOURCE: Page_70]

Farmers are proud of their achievements, positively comparing them to the services [SOURCE: Page_70]

provided by engineers: [SOURCE: Page_70]

’A year after installing drip irrigation, we noticed that the emitters got clogged after every [SOURCE: Page_70]

irrigation round, even though the company had provided disk filters. I talked to the boss [SOURCE: Page_70]

and suggested installing hydrocyclones, like the farmers of Agadir. We had them made by a [SOURCE: Page_70]

welder nearby… and installed them ourselves… bricolage was necessary to obtain a good [SOURCE: Page_70]

system. Just think about it, the company with all its engineers forgot to install [SOURCE: Page_70]

hydrocyclones!’ (Amine, farm manager, aged 45) [SOURCE: Page_70]

When some labourers who had previously worked on large farms decided to install drip [SOURCE: Page_70]

irrigation on their own small-­‐scale farms, they adapted the system to their physical and [SOURCE: Page_70]

socio-­‐economic constraints. Later on, these small innovators advised neighbours and [SOURCE: Page_70]

friends about drip irrigation, and some even became fitters or retailers. Drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_70]

spread fast to large numbers of small horticultural farmers. [SOURCE: Page_70]

Three alternative types of drip irrigation systems progressively became available on the [SOURCE: Page_70]

local market, installed by local fitters or farmers themselves. The first systems were [SOURCE: Page_70]

incomplete, because farmers intentionally left out one or more of the ’regular’ [SOURCE: Page_70]

components. This mainly concerned the fertigation and filter systems. Some farmers [SOURCE: Page_70]

replaced these with local low-­‐cost alternatives; others simply decided to do without [SOURCE: Page_70]

them as they considered them bothersome or too expensive. [SOURCE: Page_70]

Secondly, some small horticultural farmers opted for the so-­‐called roubiniyatte or valve [SOURCE: Page_70]

systems. This relatively cheap and simple system is composed of only the basic [SOURCE: Page_70]

components: engine, pump, tubing and valves. In general, there is no filter system, and a [SOURCE: Page_70]

very basic fertigation unit. The system is used for horticulture in small plots (1–3 ha), [SOURCE: Page_70]

and is particularly suitable for plots where the ground is not even, as the different drip [SOURCE: Page_70]

lines can be opened and closed according to the pressure available. Farmers who have [SOURCE: Page_70]

problems of water shortage also used valve systems, as they can irrigate the different [SOURCE: Page_70]

70 [SOURCE: Page_70]

parts of a plot one after another. However, these systems have a number of limitations [SOURCE: Page_71]

(clogging of emitters, poor fertigation, labour requirements), and farmers consider it a [SOURCE: Page_71]

transitory system. Having gained experience, many decide to purchase a more [SOURCE: Page_71]

sophisticated system, which explains why only a minority of farms (two in our sample) [SOURCE: Page_71]

use valve systems. [SOURCE: Page_71]

Thirdly, reference farmers and small innovators often first installed mobile systems. A [SOURCE: Page_71]

mobile system comprises a pumping station, a fertigation unit and a filter system. The [SOURCE: Page_71]

mainline pipe is not buried, so the farmer can move the system from place to place and [SOURCE: Page_71]

irrigate different plots using the same equipment. Tenants or lessees also use this [SOURCE: Page_71]

system. They are hesitant to invest in more permanent systems: [SOURCE: Page_71]

’I have a regular system on my own farm, and I could install it here myself. But what would [SOURCE: Page_71]

happen if tomorrow my arrangement with the landowner breaks up? I could not take [SOURCE: Page_71]

anything with me, not even the drip lines!’ (Said, lessee, aged 60) [SOURCE: Page_71]

The mobile system is used on a minority of farms (only one in our sample). As with the [SOURCE: Page_71]

valve system, it is generally abandoned after one or two irrigation seasons. It is not [SOURCE: Page_71]

expensive, and a good system to ’learn the tricks of the trade’ without many risks. [SOURCE: Page_71]

Instance 3: Integrating the subsidy procedures [SOURCE: Page_71]

The third bricolage instance involves producing systems and component parts that meet [SOURCE: Page_71]

national standards in order to qualify for state subsidies (a scheme called PNEEI— National Programme of Irrigation Water Saving). The different categories of farmers, [SOURCE: Page_71]

and the different local actors (fitters, retailers, advisers) quickly took up the opportunity [SOURCE: Page_71]

offered by state subsidies, when in 2006 it was increased to 60% of the cost of a drip [SOURCE: Page_71]

irrigation system (in 2010 it again increased to 80–100%). Previously, only the notables, [SOURCE: Page_71]

and sometimes the reference farmers had applied for subsidies. Small-­‐scale farmers [SOURCE: Page_71]

considered that the gains to be had for their small surface areas did not compensate for [SOURCE: Page_71]

the (cumbersome) procedures involved in applying for subsidies. With higher subsidies [SOURCE: Page_71]

available, more farmers managing smaller farms have engaged with the subsidy scheme. [SOURCE: Page_71]

The Moroccan standards for equipment in the subsidy procedure were similar to those [SOURCE: Page_71]

of the imported equipment, and all categories of farmers were increasingly interested in [SOURCE: Page_71]

installing the complete drip irrigation system, after they had tried alternative systems. [SOURCE: Page_71]

Interestingly, the local support sector had become sufficiently mature to propose locally [SOURCE: Page_71]

manufactured equipment as part of the subsidized drip irrigation systems, although [SOURCE: Page_71]

most of this equipment had not been tested or officially approved. However, the [SOURCE: Page_71]

standards of the subsidy procedure clearly influenced local standards: hydrocyclones [SOURCE: Page_71]

had to be red, with a better outside finish. Valves had to be orange, the colour used by a [SOURCE: Page_71]

popular brand appreciated for its quality. [SOURCE: Page_71]

## A FILTER SYSTEM CROSSING THE THREE INSTANCES OF BRICOLAGE

Another way of illustrating the bricolage types is to follow three transformations of a [SOURCE: Page_72]

sand filter, important for cleaning the source water before it enters the drip lines and [SOURCE: Page_72]

emitters. The town of Haj Kaddour is known as a centre for the purchase and repair of [SOURCE: Page_72]

farm equipment, as well as a source of innovation. Welders set up in the area in the [SOURCE: Page_72]

1980s. Their main activity was repairing motor pumps for tubewells and tractor [SOURCE: Page_72]

engines. One of the welders—Driss—a trained mechanical technician, did two years’ [SOURCE: Page_72]

apprenticeship with an established welder, learning how to repair pumps, engines and [SOURCE: Page_72]

ploughs. When he opened his own shop in 1996, as he was very dynamic he quickly [SOURCE: Page_72]

overtook his former master, gained a reputation for his skills and became the pioneer of [SOURCE: Page_72]

different local inventions. [SOURCE: Page_72]

In 1998, during the first instance of bricolage when farmers had learned to work and [SOURCE: Page_72]

maintain imported equipment, a well-­‐known farmer belonging to the notables asked [SOURCE: Page_72]

him to make him a hydrocyclone (sand filter), as he was not satisfied with the quality of [SOURCE: Page_72]

imported hydrocyclones he used. To learn how to make a hydrocyclone, and to get the [SOURCE: Page_72]

right dimensions, Driss bought an original filter in a neighbouring town. In his workshop [SOURCE: Page_72]

he dismantled it. Using a newspaper, he measured the exact dimensions of all the [SOURCE: Page_72]

components before making a cardboard pattern to cut the parts. He had purchased good [SOURCE: Page_72]

quality sheet metal, as he did not want to disappoint his customer. The hydrocyclone he [SOURCE: Page_72]

manufactured turned out to be stronger than the imported one: [SOURCE: Page_72]

’The farmer was very happy with it. He is still using it today. I did everything I could to [SOURCE: Page_72]

make a high quality filter. He suggested I should make some hydrocyclones and put them [SOURCE: Page_72]

on display. Two months later, two of his friends asked me to make them a filter each.’ [SOURCE: Page_72]

(Driss, welder, aged 40) [SOURCE: Page_72]

The first customers were large-­‐ and medium-­‐scale farmers (notables, reference farmers). [SOURCE: Page_72]

The welder paid a lot of attention to the rigidity of the sheet metal, and the satisfactory [SOURCE: Page_72]

functioning of the filter. From 2000 on, there was a sharp increase in the demand for [SOURCE: Page_72]

local fabrications. Farmers travelled 30–40 km to buy these hydrocyclones. At the time, [SOURCE: Page_72]

only Driss manufactured them, making 30–40 filters a year. To cope with demand, he [SOURCE: Page_72]

recruited young workers. The demand exploded in 2004, when farmers of all categories, [SOURCE: Page_72]

including small horticultural farmers, equipped their systems with such filters. The area [SOURCE: Page_72]

had clearly entered into the second instance of bricolage with different farmers adopting [SOURCE: Page_72]

different types of drip irrigation systems, which had been physically modified and [SOURCE: Page_72]

tailored to their needs. [SOURCE: Page_72]

From 2008 on, farmers had new requirements for locally manufactured hydrocyclones. [SOURCE: Page_72]

Not only did the hydrocyclone have to work well, it also had to look like a professional [SOURCE: Page_72]

piece of work, as these local filters were now integrated in the drip irrigation systems [SOURCE: Page_72]

subsidized by the state. In addition to the rigidity of the filter, the farmers were strict [SOURCE: Page_72]

about its finish and the visual quality of the hydrocyclone. The standards laid down in [SOURCE: Page_72]

the national subsidy procedure were consequently applied locally, but local equipment [SOURCE: Page_72]

72 [SOURCE: Page_72]

was also integrated into the subsidy procedure during the third instance of bricolage. [SOURCE: Page_73]

When asked about the origin of the hydrocyclone he produces, Driss’s proud answer is: [SOURCE: Page_73]

’I am the one who made the original.’ Even the profile of his customers changed. In [SOURCE: Page_73]

addition to farmers, retailers and fitters came to add locally manufactured filters to their [SOURCE: Page_73]

supplies. [SOURCE: Page_73]

## STANDARDIZATION OF DRIP IRRIGATION EQUIPMENT?

In the second instance of bricolage, different alternative drip irrigation systems were [SOURCE: Page_73]

designed and used. Once they had acquired some experience and, perhaps more [SOURCE: Page_73]

importantly, when they had enough money, farmers progressively opted for systems [SOURCE: Page_73]

that are referred to as rasmi systems, meaning ’regular’ or ’complete’ systems. While [SOURCE: Page_73]

using alternative (and cheaper) drip irrigation systems, they learned the importance of [SOURCE: Page_73]

the different components of a drip irrigation system. This concerned the filter system, [SOURCE: Page_73]

due to frequent problems with clogged emitters, and the fertigation system, as they [SOURCE: Page_73]

understood the importance of applying the exact amount of fertilizer and pesticide [SOURCE: Page_73]

required evenly over the field. Ease of use was also important, as the valve system [SOURCE: Page_73]

turned out to be labour-­‐intensive. Farmers often started out with a simple drip [SOURCE: Page_73]

irrigation system, stripped of its most costly components, and progressively improved it [SOURCE: Page_73]

through what they refer to as bricolage until they had acquired a rasmi system. [SOURCE: Page_73]

Imported equipment, local adaptations and subsidy procedures have all influenced the [SOURCE: Page_73]

local drip irrigation standards. The great majority (27/30 in our sample) of systems [SOURCE: Page_73]

today are referred to as rasmi, and appear to be compatible with both official procedures [SOURCE: Page_73]

and local requirements. Farmers assemble their systems, adapted to their specific [SOURCE: Page_73]

requirements, combining imported, nationally manufactured, and locally made [SOURCE: Page_73]

components, often installed by local fitters or by themselves, or in case of large-­‐scale [SOURCE: Page_73]

farmers, by irrigation companies: ’Today, when a farmer comes to see me, he already has [SOURCE: Page_73]

his rasmi system in mind, and comes here to buy the spare parts or components he [SOURCE: Page_73]

needs for his system’ (Khalid, retailer, aged 40). Rasmi systems have most of the [SOURCE: Page_73]

components of a standard drip irrigation system: pumping unit, filter unit, fertigation [SOURCE: Page_73]

unit, a basin in the case of large-­‐scale farmers. For all categories of farmers, the rasmi [SOURCE: Page_73]

system has become the ’normal’ system, i.e. the local standard. However, while at first [SOURCE: Page_73]

sight the rasmi system may appear to be similar from one farmer to another, in reality it [SOURCE: Page_73]

covers a wide range of modifications, as these systems cater to different physical and [SOURCE: Page_73]

socio-­‐economic requirements. ’Variations on a theme’ through bricolage are probably [SOURCE: Page_73]

the best way to describe it. In addition, contrary to the official standards, the local [SOURCE: Page_73]

standard evolves continuously with changes in available equipment or new [SOURCE: Page_73]

opportunities. In 2012, for instance, farmers irrigated rain-­‐fed cereals by connecting [SOURCE: Page_73]

sprinklers to their drip irrigation systems, despite the difference in pressure required by [SOURCE: Page_73]

sprinkler and drip irrigation systems: bricolage saved the wheat harvest. [SOURCE: Page_73]

73 [SOURCE: Page_73]

Rasmi can thus be read as a localization and flexibilization of formal standards for drip [SOURCE: Page_74]

irrigation. ISO standards inspired the formulation of the 2007 Moroccan official [SOURCE: Page_74]

standards (Laiti et al., 2005). These standards serve to test drip irrigation equipment [SOURCE: Page_74]

that is imported or fabricated in Morocco and check if it conforms to Moroccan [SOURCE: Page_74]

standards. Only equipment that conforms to these standards is eligible for subsidies. Yet, [SOURCE: Page_74]

and interestingly, in actual practice these standards do not play a large role in installing [SOURCE: Page_74]

or manufacturing equipment or in granting subsidies. Local administrators in charge of [SOURCE: Page_74]

subsidy procedures adopt a flexible attitude, because they do not want to curb the [SOURCE: Page_74]

current dynamism around drip irrigation. They believe that the standards will be [SOURCE: Page_74]

progressively applied: [SOURCE: Page_74]

’In the subsidy procedure, we pay a lot of attention to the test reports of the tubing, and to [SOURCE: Page_74]

the design study. As far as the other items, the meter, the filter system, are concerned we [SOURCE: Page_74]

are less strict, as it is not clear yet. It is the beginning of the project.’ (Mostafa, agricultural [SOURCE: Page_74]

administration, aged 40) [SOURCE: Page_74]

This flexible attitude of local administrators is important for local creativity and [SOURCE: Page_74]

bricolage to thrive, and thus partly explains the rapid spread of drip irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_74]

## ACCEPTING BRICOLAGE AS A VALID PRACTICE

The general meaning of bricolage in French has a somewhat negative connotation. The [SOURCE: Page_74]

dictionary qualifies it as nonprofessional activity, or as work that is not serious. [SOURCE: Page_74]

Although most local actors (engineers, farmers, labourers, welders) frequently practise [SOURCE: Page_74]

bricolage and refer to the term, they appreciate it differently and have given new (and [SOURCE: Page_74]

sometimes) positive meanings to the term. [SOURCE: Page_74]

Many of the reference farmers refer to bricolage as something they did in the past, when [SOURCE: Page_74]

they did not have the know-­‐how or the means to operate and maintain a drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_74]

system: [SOURCE: Page_74]

’At the beginning, I used bricolage when I installed drip irrigation, it was mobile, and I [SOURCE: Page_74]

equipped only part of the farm… I then changed the filter systems with the help of a local [SOURCE: Page_74]

welder, until I had a rasmi system. Today, there is no room for bricolage in my farm.’ [SOURCE: Page_74]

(Ahmed, farmer, aged 40) [SOURCE: Page_74]

Farmers like Ahmed consider bricolage as something belonging to a less progressive and [SOURCE: Page_74]

modern era; they are proud of the fact that they no longer practise bricolage. [SOURCE: Page_74]

Similarly, certain farmers (notables, reference farmers) use the term bricolage in a [SOURCE: Page_74]

slightly demeaning way to denote what other—whom they consider as less progressive [SOURCE: Page_74]

or advanced—farmers do. Ahmed, for instance, declares: ’Bricolage is done by farmers [SOURCE: Page_74]

who have roubiniyatte systems.’ By associating bricolage with those farmers who (still) [SOURCE: Page_74]

use valve systems, Ahmed distinguishes himself as better and more modern. Large-­‐scale [SOURCE: Page_74]

74 [SOURCE: Page_74]

farmers likewise are keen to disassociate their high-­‐tech systems (and themselves) from [SOURCE: Page_75]

bricolage. For them, bricolage denotes a deviation from the technical standards, from [SOURCE: Page_75]

what is technically most advanced. Proud to show his automated drip irrigation system [SOURCE: Page_75]

to the main author, an engineer, another farmer states: ’Come to my farm if you want to [SOURCE: Page_75]

see real drip irrigation. The equipment you usually see is just bricolage’ (Yacine, farmer, [SOURCE: Page_75]

aged 60). Yet, even on the farms of the farmers who see bricolage as something negative, [SOURCE: Page_75]

we observed adaptations of different components of the drip irrigation systems, often [SOURCE: Page_75]

manufactured locally. Contrary to the farmers themselves, their farm managers and [SOURCE: Page_75]

labourers refer to this as bricolage. They experienced that the original hightech systems [SOURCE: Page_75]

were too complicated, and consequently adapted the equipment to make it more [SOURCE: Page_75]

suitable for use. [SOURCE: Page_75]

A radically different view of bricolage can be observed among the (young) small [SOURCE: Page_75]

horticultural farmers. They often use the term proudly, acknowledging it as an [SOURCE: Page_75]

important part of their identity and daily life: ’Oh, you know, we practise bricolage all the [SOURCE: Page_75]

time, and in all areas’ (Younes, farmer, aged 30). Bricolage is what is needed to deal with [SOURCE: Page_75]

change, and with domesticating the technique: ’Bricolage helped me to obtain a modern [SOURCE: Page_75]

drip irrigation system’ (Omar, farmer, aged 37). It is very much linked to the technology: [SOURCE: Page_75]

’Before, when we used water from the canal, we could not practise bricolage, there were [SOURCE: Page_75]

no valves’ (Amine, farmer, aged 26). These young farmers use the term to indicate their [SOURCE: Page_75]

technical prowess and their capacity to keep up with rapid changes. For them, bricolage [SOURCE: Page_75]

is a sign of a modern, advanced way of doing agriculture, which is changing rapidly: ’We [SOURCE: Page_75]

are obliged to develop our agriculture…when you practise bricolage, you advance your [SOURCE: Page_75]

agriculture, and you can obtain drip irrigation’ (Younes, farmer, aged 30). Bricolage is no [SOURCE: Page_75]

longer only linked to drip irrigation, but to farming in general. Through bricolage, young [SOURCE: Page_75]

people even manage to take control of the family farm, as the parents find it difficult to [SOURCE: Page_75]

adapt to rapid changes. The use of the word bricolage marks a distinction between [SOURCE: Page_75]

different generations of farmers. For older ones, agriculture is a noble and precious craft [SOURCE: Page_75]

typified by continuity: ’We never practise bricolage, agriculture is sacred. It is in building [SOURCE: Page_75]

and construction that people use bricolage’ (Mohamed, farmer, aged 60). For them, [SOURCE: Page_75]

bricolage is a pejorative term, denoting work that is not properly scheduled. When they [SOURCE: Page_75]

talk about drip irrigation, they seem to know little about the expertise required by those [SOURCE: Page_75]

who daily manage drip irrigation, generally their sons. [SOURCE: Page_75]

Interestingly, engineers have two ways of referring to bricolage. When we presented this [SOURCE: Page_75]

study to engineering students, their reactions were telling: ’this is the problem of [SOURCE: Page_75]

Morocco’. Bricolage for them was a synonym for amateurism, resulting in systems that [SOURCE: Page_75]

deviate from technical specifications and norms. When we interviewed engineers [SOURCE: Page_75]

working for consultancy firms, or irrigation companies in their offices, they echoed this [SOURCE: Page_75]

view: ’we never practise bricolage, we do serious work here’. However, during [SOURCE: Page_75]

discussions with them in the field, or when referring to concrete cases, engineers [SOURCE: Page_75]

showed a different attitude. Bricolage was indispensable if one wanted to stay in [SOURCE: Page_75]

business. These engineers collaborate with local fitters and retailers on the design and [SOURCE: Page_75]

installation of drip irrigation equipment: ’We work more and more with local fitters, I [SOURCE: Page_75]

75 [SOURCE: Page_75]

am obliged to practise bricolage. They are there, they know all the farmers’ (Hassan, [SOURCE: Page_76]

engineer, aged 42). [SOURCE: Page_76]

## DISCUSSION

Bricolage as a creative process of learning and adaptation [SOURCE: Page_76]

In this study, we showed how bricolage changed users’ context and the drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_76]

technology itself (and even its meaning), through a creative process of learning and [SOURCE: Page_76]

adaptation. We described three instances of bricolage. Local users first learned how to [SOURCE: Page_76]

make imported equipment work, then deconstructed drip irrigation systems and [SOURCE: Page_76]

designed alternative low-­‐cost systems, and finally selected and designed ’regular’ [SOURCE: Page_76]

systems. Local components found their way into the subsidized programmes, which, [SOURCE: Page_76]

thanks to their flexibility, progressively incorporated local makes. In the process, [SOURCE: Page_76]

different actors gradually learned about drip technology while adapting it to their own [SOURCE: Page_76]

situation, calling on a support sector that could be termed part of a ’grey area’. Similarly, [SOURCE: Page_76]

Vermillion (1989) considered the design process of irrigation systems interactive, [SOURCE: Page_76]

admitting a ’double loop learning’, which incorporates local knowledge at the system [SOURCE: Page_76]

level, and permits the questioning of design criteria. [SOURCE: Page_76]

The distributed innovation agency of the drip irrigation community (farmers, fitters, [SOURCE: Page_76]

welders, engineers) in this study bears a certain resemblance to the process of bricolage [SOURCE: Page_76]

described by Garud and Karnøe (2003) on windmills in Denmark. Drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_76]

systems were adapted by a wide range of different actors, making them accessible to a [SOURCE: Page_76]

wide range of users, and, in so doing, they simultaneously created local experience and [SOURCE: Page_76]

expertise and increased the demand for drip irrigation. The flexibility of local [SOURCE: Page_76]

administrators dealing with subsidy programmes was important, as they allowed [SOURCE: Page_76]

incorporation of local fabrications in subsidized systems. This helped drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_76]

innovation to become embedded in the industrial process, with certain welders even [SOURCE: Page_76]

evolving as major players. [SOURCE: Page_76]

Engineers as ’bricoleurs’? [SOURCE: Page_76]

The descriptions of bricolage and the fact that it involves so many different actors may [SOURCE: Page_76]

seem to indicate that engineers, who are classically responsible for the design and [SOURCE: Page_76]

installation of such high-­‐tech equipment, are no longer necessary. Indeed, engineers [SOURCE: Page_76]

when confronted with instances of bricolage relate this to unwanted and suboptimal [SOURCE: Page_76]

deviations from the ideal and uniform design as expressed in international standards. [SOURCE: Page_76]

Using Lévi-­‐Strauss’s concept of bricolage, Pascon (1973) suggested that once confronted [SOURCE: Page_76]

with field realities, such as the drip irrigation systems described in this study, the [SOURCE: Page_76]

engineer has two options. Either he adapts his ideal and scholarly models to field [SOURCE: Page_76]

realities, and engages in a process that can be termed bricolage, or he continues to stick [SOURCE: Page_76]

to official design procedures and norms. When choosing the first option, making do with [SOURCE: Page_76]

whatever is available locally and designing systems that work, the engineers and their [SOURCE: Page_76]

designs may be well received by end-­‐users, but this is often a problem for his peers, i.e. [SOURCE: Page_76]

76 [SOURCE: Page_76]

the engineering community. The second option will have the reverse effect: it will create [SOURCE: Page_77]

sophisticated systems that do not work, but impose respect among peers. Similarly, [SOURCE: Page_77]

Popper argued for a qualification of the work of engineers as ’piecemeal engineering’ [SOURCE: Page_77]

(the meaning of which comes close to bricolage), both to temper expectations about the [SOURCE: Page_77]

transformational capacities of technology and to better appreciate what the actual work [SOURCE: Page_77]

that engineers are involved in consists of (Popper, 1961). [SOURCE: Page_77]

Interestingly, our study shows that though engineers tend to regard bricolage [SOURCE: Page_77]

negatively, the descriptions of Pascon and Popper do in fact apply to what many of them [SOURCE: Page_77]

do in their day-­‐to-­‐day practices. Engineers have shown their ability to engage in [SOURCE: Page_77]

bricolage, working with local fitters, farmers and farm labourers to make technologically [SOURCE: Page_77]

advanced international equipment fit with local markets and fields. This is of course in [SOURCE: Page_77]

line with more philosophical reflections on science and engineering, underlining the fact [SOURCE: Page_77]

that bricolage is intrinsic to the actual practices of scientists and engineers (Derrida, [SOURCE: Page_77]

1978). [SOURCE: Page_77]

## CONCLUSIONS

We have described instances of bricolage as practised by farmers and other actors when [SOURCE: Page_77]

drip irrigation is being ’recontextualised’ for a wide array of farms, ranging from large [SOURCE: Page_77]

commercial farm enterprises to smaller farms in the Saiss (Morocco). We conclude that [SOURCE: Page_77]

the term bricolage is an interesting way of capturing and explaining processes of [SOURCE: Page_77]

technological change. Awareness of the existence of bricolage means acknowledging that [SOURCE: Page_77]

these processes are not straightforward, linear and rational, with farmers gradually [SOURCE: Page_77]

learning how to use a new technology according to the specifications (and standards) of [SOURCE: Page_77]

engineers. It shows that, instead, innovation is a messy, largely unpredictable and [SOURCE: Page_77]

creative process that consists of iterative interactions and negotiations between [SOURCE: Page_77]

farmers, engineers and many other actors. This process may lead to new, dynamic and [SOURCE: Page_77]

localized standards for the technology, while institutionalizing bricolage. [SOURCE: Page_77]

Understanding technological innovation as bricolage effectively draws attention away [SOURCE: Page_77]

from the technology itself to the practices through which different actors mobilize ideas, [SOURCE: Page_77]

materials and other actors to adjust the new technology to their specific situations and [SOURCE: Page_77]

to make it work for them. Rather than taking the ’official’ design as the norm against [SOURCE: Page_77]

which those that have been the subject of bricolage are measured and judged, the [SOURCE: Page_77]

concept of bricolage thus acknowledges that technologies always require modification [SOURCE: Page_77]

and adaptation to make them usable in specific environmental and user contexts. As the [SOURCE: Page_77]

examples described here suggest, doing this successfully requires both the engineer’s [SOURCE: Page_77]

specialized knowledge of crop–water–plan relations and ways to make water flow from [SOURCE: Page_77]

one place to another and knowledge of the users’ particular situations. Bricolage [SOURCE: Page_77]

therefore entails and depends on (negotiated) alliances between engineers and farmers. [SOURCE: Page_77]

Processes of bricolage multiply the number of possible drip systems, with the specifics [SOURCE: Page_77]

of the system depending both on the degree of familiarity of a farmer with the [SOURCE: Page_77]

technology, and on the objectives the farmers wish to pursue by using it: save labour, [SOURCE: Page_77]

77 [SOURCE: Page_77]

extend their irrigated land, increase yields or save water. The resulting variety of [SOURCE: Page_78]

systems helps us to understand why drip irrigation can ’do’ something different in each [SOURCE: Page_78]

different context. Indeed, the fact that drip irrigation lends itself to many adjustments, [SOURCE: Page_78]

the fact that it is variable and multiple, may well be a key factor explaining why it is [SOURCE: Page_78]

spreading so fast and why so many farmers are enthusiastic about it. [SOURCE: Page_78]

L’irrigation au goutte à goutte est promue de par le monde pour sa capacité d’améliorer [SOURCE: Page_80]

la productivité de l'eau (Luquet et al., 2005). Ceci est basé d'abord sur l'affirmation que [SOURCE: Page_80]

l'irrigation au goutte à goutte est beaucoup plus efficiente, avec une efficience théorique [SOURCE: Page_80]

pouvant atteindre 90 à 95% (Pénadille, 1998) comparée aux autres techniques [SOURCE: Page_80]

d'irrigation tels que le gravitaire ou l'irrigation par aspersion. Moins d'eau est perdue [SOURCE: Page_80]

dans le transport, et l'eau est appliquée directement au pied de la plante, économisant [SOURCE: Page_80]

ainsi de l'eau l’irrigation. Deuxièmement, l'irrigation au goutte à goutte permet une [SOURCE: Page_80]

distribution plus uniforme de l'eau à travers le champ (c'est à dire toutes les plantes [SOURCE: Page_80]

reçoivent presque la même quantité d'eau) et facilite l'application de l'eau (facilité [SOURCE: Page_80]

d'utilisation, une fréquence plus élevée d'irrigation) (Ayars et al, 2006), ce qui améliore [SOURCE: Page_80]

le rendement des cultures (Wu et Gitlin 1983 ; Wang et al, 2013). Au Maroc, le [SOURCE: Page_80]

programme national d’économie d’eau en irrigation (PNEEI) prévoit une amélioration [SOURCE: Page_80]

de l’efficience d’irrigation à la parcelle -­‐ de 50% en irrigation gravitaire vers 90% en [SOURCE: Page_80]

irrigation localisée -­‐ pour économiser 826 millions de m^3/an. Nous avons alors procédé [SOURCE: Page_80]

à une série de mesures de performances d’irrigation afin de vérifier si cette efficience [SOURCE: Page_80]

théorique se matérialise sur le terrain quand la technologie interagit avec les acteurs [SOURCE: Page_80]

locaux et son environnement physique (nature des sols, pente des parcelles) et socio-­‐ économique (orientations de l’exploitation agricole, par exemple). Ainsi, nous avons [SOURCE: Page_80]

développé une méthodologie pour mettre en relation les performances d’irrigation [SOURCE: Page_80]

mesurées en conditions réelles et les pratiques et logiques des utilisateurs. Nous [SOURCE: Page_80]

postulons que les performances d’irrigation ne dépendent pas uniquement des [SOURCE: Page_80]

caractéristiques techniques du goutte à goutte, mais dépendent surtout de ce que les [SOURCE: Page_80]

utilisateurs font du goutte à goutte. En effet, ce sont eux qui déterminent la performance [SOURCE: Page_80]

d’irrigation du goutte à goutte, par leurs pratiques d’irrigation liées à leurs logiques et [SOURCE: Page_80]

attentes dans l’utilisation du goutte à goutte. [SOURCE: Page_80]

A travers ce chapitre, nous allons contribuer aux débats scientifiques autours des [SOURCE: Page_80]

performances d’irrigation liées au goutte à goutte. En effet, nous avons développé une [SOURCE: Page_80]

approche méthodologique originale pour déterminer les performances d’irrigation in [SOURCE: Page_80]

situ et expliquer la diversité des performances d’irrigation mesurées sur le terrain. Nous [SOURCE: Page_80]

considérons l’utilisateur comme acteur principal responsable des performances [SOURCE: Page_80]

d’irrigation. Mettre l’utilisateur au centre des débats scientifiques autour de la question [SOURCE: Page_80]

d’économie d’eau permettrait de le rendre plus visible, et par conséquent, conduirait à [SOURCE: Page_80]

80 [SOURCE: Page_80]

une évaluation plus réaliste des performances d’irrigation et attirerait l'attention des [SOURCE: Page_81]

décideurs politiques sur les conditions réelles dans lesquelles l’irrigation au goutte à [SOURCE: Page_81]

goutte est utilisée sur le terrain. [SOURCE: Page_81]

Dans ce chapitre nous présentons en premier les résultats de mesures de performances [SOURCE: Page_81]

d’irrigation, à travers deux indicateurs : l’efficience d’irrigation (EI) et le coefficient [SOURCE: Page_81]

d’uniformité (CU). Nous analysons en deuxième lieu les pratiques d’irrigation et on [SOURCE: Page_81]

termine par déterminer les logiques des utilisateurs responsables de l’utilisation du [SOURCE: Page_81]

goutte à goutte. [SOURCE: Page_81]

81 [SOURCE: Page_81]

Making the user visible: analysing irrigation practices and farmers’ logic to [SOURCE: Page_82]

explain actual drip irrigation performance [SOURCE: Page_82]

Maya Benounichea,b, Marcel Kupera,b, Ali Hammanib, Harm Boesveldc [SOURCE: Page_82]

aCIRAD, UMR G-­‐EAU, F-­‐34398 Montpellier, France [SOURCE: Page_82]

bInstitut Agronomique et Vétérinaire Hassan II, Rabat, Maroc [SOURCE: Page_82]

cWater Resources Management, Environmental Science Group, Wageningen University, [SOURCE: Page_82]

The Netherlands [SOURCE: Page_82]

Corresponding author: Maya Benouniche [SOURCE: Page_82]

+212 6 24 51 46 31 [SOURCE: Page_82]

Irrigation Science: Volume 32, Issue 6 (2014), Page 405-­‐420 [SOURCE: Page_82]

Abstract [SOURCE: Page_82]

The actual performance of drip irrigation (irrigation efficiency, distribution uniformity) [SOURCE: Page_82]

in the field is often quite different from that obtained in experimental stations. We [SOURCE: Page_82]

developed an approach to explain the actual irrigation performance of drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_82]

systems by linking measured performances to farmers’ irrigation practices, and these [SOURCE: Page_82]

practices to the underlying logic of farmers who operate these systems. This approach [SOURCE: Page_82]

was applied to 22 farms in Morocco. Four sets of variables helped explain the gap [SOURCE: Page_82]

between the actual irrigation performance and the performance obtained in [SOURCE: Page_82]

experimental conditions: 1) farmers have agro-­‐economic motivations, or want to [SOURCE: Page_82]

improve their social status, and for them, irrigation performance is at best an [SOURCE: Page_82]

intermediate objective. 2) Irrigation performance is not a static value, but a rapidly [SOURCE: Page_82]

evolving process, related to the (perceived) ability of farmers to change irrigation [SOURCE: Page_82]

practices, and renew irrigation equipment, but also to farmers’ aspirations. 3) The social [SOURCE: Page_82]

network of farmers, supporting the introduction and use of drip irrigation, determines [SOURCE: Page_82]

how farmers may share experience, information, and know-­‐how related to drip [SOURCE: Page_82]

irrigation. 4) Today, there is no social pressure to irrigate carefully to save water; only [SOURCE: Page_82]

the state explicitly links the use of drip irrigation to saving water. Making the drip user [SOURCE: Page_82]

visible in research and policy studies would lead to more realistic assessments of [SOURCE: Page_82]

irrigation performance, and draw the attention of policy makers to the actual conditions [SOURCE: Page_82]

in which drip irrigation is used, and as a consequence help incorporate ‘saving water’ as [SOURCE: Page_82]

an objective for drip irrigation users. [SOURCE: Page_82]

Key words [SOURCE: Page_82]

irrigation; performance; irrigation practices; drip; users; Morocco [SOURCE: Page_82]

82 [SOURCE: Page_82]

### 1. Introduction

Drip irrigation is widely promoted as a way of improving water productivity (Luquet et [SOURCE: Page_83]

al. 2005). This is based firstly on the assertion that drip irrigation is much more efficient [SOURCE: Page_83]

than other irrigation techniques such as furrow or sprinkler irrigation. Less water is lost [SOURCE: Page_83]

in conveyance, and water is applied directly in the immediate vicinity of the plant, [SOURCE: Page_83]

thereby saving water. Secondly, drip irrigation allows more uniform distribution of [SOURCE: Page_83]

water across the field (i.e. all plants receive almost the same quantity of water), and [SOURCE: Page_83]

facilitates the application of water (ease of use, higher irrigation frequency), thereby [SOURCE: Page_83]

improving crop yields (Wu and Gitlin 1983; Wang et al. 2013). Since drip irrigation is [SOURCE: Page_83]

almost invariably associated with the application of nutrients (fertigation), these [SOURCE: Page_83]

systems also allow better nutrient management. This led certain authors to claim that [SOURCE: Page_83]

drip irrigation may double or triple the water productivity (Postel, 2000; Gleick, 2002). [SOURCE: Page_83]

However, recent papers are more cautious about water efficiency claims of drip [SOURCE: Page_83]

irrigation, and pinpointed the fact that the claimed impacts of this technology are rarely [SOURCE: Page_83]

compared with actual practice (Lankford, 2012; van der Kooij et al. 2013). Other authors [SOURCE: Page_83]

point to potentially negative effects of drip irrigation on the environment (salinization, [SOURCE: Page_83]

groundwater pollution), when drip irrigation systems and irrigation practices are not [SOURCE: Page_83]

tailored to their environment (Cote et al. 2003). Another line of criticism relates to the [SOURCE: Page_83]

scale at which water productivity is measured. While drip irrigation systems may save [SOURCE: Page_83]

water at the plot level, this may not be the case at the basin level, where water that is [SOURCE: Page_83]

lost in one location is used in another (Molden et al. 2003; van Halsema and Vincent, [SOURCE: Page_83]

2012). We argue in this paper that even at the plot level, the use of drip irrigation may [SOURCE: Page_83]

not lead to water saving. [SOURCE: Page_83]

Generally, the “goodness” of irrigation performance is related to two types of indicators: [SOURCE: Page_83]

irrigation efficiency, and the uniform distribution of water throughout the plot (Burt et [SOURCE: Page_83]

al. 1997). Many indicators related to the two terms were developed to determine [SOURCE: Page_83]

irrigation performance (Burt et al. 1997). These were mostly applied to irrigation [SOURCE: Page_83]

systems in experimental stations to test the technical parameters of drip irrigation (e.g. [SOURCE: Page_83]

hydraulic properties of drippers) (Camp et al. 1997; van der Kooij et al. 2013). However, [SOURCE: Page_83]

in farmers’ fields, irrigation performance depends not only on the technical standards of [SOURCE: Page_83]

irrigation equipment, but also on farmers’ irrigation practices, as well as on the [SOURCE: Page_83]

maintenance of the equipment (Tanji and Keyes 2002; Luquet et al. 2005). Wolf et al. [SOURCE: Page_83]

(1995) demonstrated in Jordan, that the efficiency of drip irrigation was even lower than [SOURCE: Page_83]

the efficiency of surface irrigation due to poor maintenance, inadequate use of [SOURCE: Page_83]

equipment, and poor irrigation practices. Slatni et al. (2004) reported similar findings in [SOURCE: Page_83]

the case of Tunisia. In this paper we argue that irrigation practices depend, in turn, on [SOURCE: Page_83]

the larger objectives and constraints of farmers. This may explain the wide range of [SOURCE: Page_83]

hydraulic performance reported in the rare studies carried out in real-­‐life settings (Wolf [SOURCE: Page_83]

et al. 1995; Vidal et al. 2001). The actual irrigation performance of drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_83]

systems is an important issue, as it will determine both the real water savings and the [SOURCE: Page_83]

final agricultural production that will ultimately be obtained. [SOURCE: Page_83]

83 [SOURCE: Page_83]

Leeuwis and van den Ban (2004) proposed a model to understand the social nature and [SOURCE: Page_84]

the logic underlying farmers’ (technical) practices. This logic is determined by variables [SOURCE: Page_84]

related to the perceived (technical, economic, social) consequences of changing practices [SOURCE: Page_84]

and their assessment by individual farmers, farmers’ perceived ability to carry out these [SOURCE: Page_84]

(changes in) practices in the long run, the wider social environment that will support (or [SOURCE: Page_84]

not) such practices, and perceived social relationships and pressure. This model was [SOURCE: Page_84]

applied to different case studies, one example being soil fertility management practices [SOURCE: Page_84]

(Adjei-­‐Nsiah et al. 2004). [SOURCE: Page_84]

We hypothesized that the indicators proposed in the literature are useful to qualify the [SOURCE: Page_84]

performance of drip irrigation, but insufficient to analyze the reasons for the differences [SOURCE: Page_84]

and dynamics in the actual irrigation performance in the field. In the present study, we [SOURCE: Page_84]

developed and tested an approach to explain the performance of drip irrigation systems [SOURCE: Page_84]

observed in the field, by linking measured performances to farmers’ actual irrigation [SOURCE: Page_84]

practices, and to the underlying logic of farmers who own and/or operate these systems. [SOURCE: Page_84]

This work answers calls of different authors, who advocated applying multidisciplinary [SOURCE: Page_84]

approaches for a sound comprehension of irrigation performance (van Schilfgaarde, [SOURCE: Page_84]

1994; Tanji and Keyes, 2002; van der Kooij et al. 2013). [SOURCE: Page_84]
