### 2. Methodology

2.1 Study area [SOURCE: Page_84]

The study area is located in the Saiss plain between the cities of Meknes and Fes in [SOURCE: Page_84]

northwest Morocco, which is a rich agricultural area (Berriane, 2002). The farms are [SOURCE: Page_84]

located in the vicinity of the small town of Ain Taoujdat. In the past, the farming systems [SOURCE: Page_84]

in the Saiss were characterized by rain fed agriculture (cereals, vineyards), and by some [SOURCE: Page_84]

irrigated crops (tobacco, orchards, horticulture) in a number of small irrigation schemes [SOURCE: Page_84]

irrigated by springs. Due to the droughts that occurred in the 1980s and the arrival of [SOURCE: Page_84]

investors, farmers progressively, but massively turned to the use of groundwater [SOURCE: Page_84]

through individual wells and tube wells. The river basin agency estimates the current [SOURCE: Page_84]

irrigated area to be 37,000 ha, of which 25,000 ha are irrigated by groundwater through [SOURCE: Page_84]

more than 12,000 individual (tube)wells, most of which are not officially registered. [SOURCE: Page_84]

Access to groundwater led to a sharp increase in the area under orchards (42% of the [SOURCE: Page_84]

irrigated area) and horticulture (35%). According to the river basin agency, a little over [SOURCE: Page_84]

half of the irrigated area is now irrigated by drip irrigation. This figure probably applies [SOURCE: Page_84]

only to the area irrigated by groundwater, as so far, drip irrigation is not used on a large [SOURCE: Page_84]

scale in the small-­‐scale gravity irrigation schemes. There is an increasing pressure on [SOURCE: Page_84]

groundwater resources in the Saiss, and it is often claimed that drip irrigation may [SOURCE: Page_84]

reduce the volume of water used for agriculture through higher irrigation efficiency at [SOURCE: Page_84]

the plot level. This prompted us to investigate the irrigation performance of drip [SOURCE: Page_84]

irrigation systems in farmer’s fields. [SOURCE: Page_84]

84 [SOURCE: Page_84]

We conducted our study in the 2011 irrigation season (March to October) on 22 drip [SOURCE: Page_85]

irrigation systems in horticultural plots on 22 Moroccan farms, representative of the [SOURCE: Page_85]

diversity of horticultural farms in the area. We first conducted an exploratory study on [SOURCE: Page_85]

the different farm types, and drip irrigation systems in the area. We then used different [SOURCE: Page_85]

criteria to select the farms and plots, using a stratified random sample approach (table [SOURCE: Page_85]

1). Firstly, only horticultural plots were selected (onion, potatoes, and seed potatoes), so [SOURCE: Page_85]

we would be able to compare irrigation practices; irrigation of fruit orchards is different [SOURCE: Page_85]

(larger plots, year round irrigation), and so we excluded orchards from this study. [SOURCE: Page_85]

Secondly, we selected a wide range of farms, in terms of size (from 1 ha to 120 ha; table [SOURCE: Page_85]

1), as we hypothesized that farm size had an impact on the choice and quality of the [SOURCE: Page_85]

irrigation equipment (as large-­‐scale farms have more financial means), and on irrigation [SOURCE: Page_85]

practices (larger plots, fewer financial constraints for larger farms). Thirdly, we used the [SOURCE: Page_85]

installation date to select irrigation equipment of different age, hypothesizing that older [SOURCE: Page_85]

equipment would have a lower irrigation performance (table 1). The access to water on [SOURCE: Page_85]

the vast majority of farms is provided by wells of about 35-­‐45 m depth. The flow rate of [SOURCE: Page_85]

these wells ranges from 4-­‐8 l/s. The cost of a fully equipped well (excavation cost, pump, [SOURCE: Page_85]

engine) of 40 m ranged from 4,400 to 6,200 euros. Only, the large-­‐scale farmers in this [SOURCE: Page_85]

area had tubewells of more than 85 m depth, generally with a flow rate of 7-­‐8 l/s. The [SOURCE: Page_85]

cost of a fully equipped tubewell (drilling cost, pump, engine) with a depth of 100 m [SOURCE: Page_85]

ranged from 5,000 to 9,600 euros. The cost of a drip irrigation system ranged from 2,500 [SOURCE: Page_85]

to 4,500 €/ha, depending on the quality of the installation, and the components of a [SOURCE: Page_85]

particular drip irrigation system (Ameur et al, 2013). [SOURCE: Page_85]

These costs can be compared with the gains obtained by farmers from, for instance, the [SOURCE: Page_85]

production of onions. According to a recent economic study in the area on the onion sub-­‐ sector, the price at a nearby wholesale market ranged from 0.1-­‐0.8 €/kg during the [SOURCE: Page_85]

period 2008-­‐2012 (Courilleau and Lejars, 2013). Farmers share the gains with a large [SOURCE: Page_85]

number of intermediaries, which take charge of the harvest and post-­‐harvest handling. [SOURCE: Page_85]

Depending on the price, but also the yields obtained (generally 80-­‐120 tons/ha), farmers [SOURCE: Page_85]

obtain margins in the range of -­‐10 to +100 €/ton (Courilleau and Lejars, 2013). In [SOURCE: Page_85]

theory, it takes thus one excellent cropping season (high yields, high prices) for a farmer [SOURCE: Page_85]

to pay back his irrigation equipment. However, the risks are high, and farmers generally [SOURCE: Page_85]

state “one year we win, one year we lose”. [SOURCE: Page_85]

In a previous survey in Morocco, we had observed differences in equipment, such as the [SOURCE: Page_85]

absence of filter systems (Benouniche et al. 2011). We, therefore, described the drip [SOURCE: Page_85]

irrigation equipment in detail for each farm, as we hypothesized that these differences [SOURCE: Page_85]

could explain part of the irrigation performance. [SOURCE: Page_85]

2.2 Research approach [SOURCE: Page_85]

Following the objectives of this study, we measured the irrigation performance at the [SOURCE: Page_85]

plot level, observed the irrigation practices of farmers and labourers, and finally we [SOURCE: Page_85]

analyzed the underlying logic of farmers operating the drip irrigation systems. [SOURCE: Page_85]

85 [SOURCE: Page_85]

We used two classic indicators to measure irrigation performance at the plot level; [SOURCE: Page_86]

irrigation efficiency (IE) and the uniformity of water distribution (DU). We determined [SOURCE: Page_86]

the irrigation efficiency, following Burt et al. (1997), who considered that “at the heart of [SOURCE: Page_86]

any consideration of irrigation performance is an irrigation-­‐water balance and [SOURCE: Page_86]

determination of the fate of various fractions of the total irrigation water applied”. This [SOURCE: Page_86]

indicator is, therefore, an interesting way of analyzing farmers’ irrigation practices. The [SOURCE: Page_86]

distribution uniformity is a standard indicator for irrigation, and even more so for drip [SOURCE: Page_86]

irrigation which aims specifically at attaining high distribution uniformity in a specific [SOURCE: Page_86]

plot, i.e. greater than 90% (Keller and Karmeli, 1974; Burt et al. 1997). Keller and [SOURCE: Page_86]

Karmeli (1974) specify that high distribution uniformity is achieved with “precision [SOURCE: Page_86]

manufacturing, sufficient filtering to eliminate clogging and uniform topography”. By [SOURCE: Page_86]

inversing this argument, we argue that the measurement of the actual DU in field [SOURCE: Page_86]

conditions – our hypothesis being that actual values of DU will be considerably different [SOURCE: Page_86]

from the projected 90% -­‐ will provide interesting information on the actual state of the [SOURCE: Page_86]

equipment, and thus on operational and maintenance practice (e.g. cleaning of filters, [SOURCE: Page_86]

renewing tubing), and on the design of the drip irrigation systems with respect to field [SOURCE: Page_86]

conditions (e.g. topography). [SOURCE: Page_86]

Irrigation efficiency (IE) is generally linked to “fractions of the irrigation water volume [SOURCE: Page_86]

that are destined for certain functions”, including transpiration, evaporation, infiltration [SOURCE: Page_86]

etc., (Burt et al. 1997). But it is also important to establish the spatial scale at which IE is [SOURCE: Page_86]

measured. In our case, IE was defined as the ratio between the volume delivered at the [SOURCE: Page_86]

entry of the plot, and the net irrigation water requirements (i.e., according to the FAO, [SOURCE: Page_86]

the difference between crop evapotranspiration and effective precipitation), both [SOURCE: Page_86]

expressed in mm. Evapotranspiration was calculated using the FAO CropWat package, [SOURCE: Page_86]

and climatic data from weather stations located in the immediate vicinity of the farms [SOURCE: Page_86]

(rainfall data came from Ain Taoujdate, all the other parameters came from Meknes [SOURCE: Page_86]

synoptic station). The exact sowing and harvesting dates were recorded for each plot, [SOURCE: Page_86]

and the soil types were defined after analysis in a soil laboratory. The soils were [SOURCE: Page_86]

generally medium-­‐textured soils (table 1). The volume of water delivered to the plots [SOURCE: Page_86]

was determined by measuring the discharge during the irrigation season, and by [SOURCE: Page_86]

checking the irrigation calendar with the farmers during interviews. We cross-­‐checked [SOURCE: Page_86]

the information they gave us through regular visits to their plots during the irrigation [SOURCE: Page_86]

season. This enabled us to detect exceptions to the (regular) irrigation schedule. We [SOURCE: Page_86]

measured the uniformity of water distribution to the entire field through water [SOURCE: Page_86]

distribution uniformity (DU), which Pereira et al. (2002) defined as the “ratio among the [SOURCE: Page_86]

average infiltrated depths in the low quarter of the field and in the entire field, both [SOURCE: Page_86]

expressed in mm.” In each plot, we measured three times the discharge of 16 drippers [SOURCE: Page_86]

equally spaced from head to tail along four different lateral lines (Keller and Karmeli, [SOURCE: Page_86]

1974). [SOURCE: Page_86]

86 [SOURCE: Page_86]

Regular visits to the plots enabled us to observe farmers’ practices, both concerning [SOURCE: Page_87]

irrigation itself and maintenance of the irrigation equipment. To this end, we used a [SOURCE: Page_87]

checklist (who irrigates when? Who monitors the irrigation performance, and how? [SOURCE: Page_87]

What maintenance is undertaken, by whom? etc.). While we took the utmost care to [SOURCE: Page_87]

record the exact timing of irrigation for each plot in our sample, the (field) conditions of [SOURCE: Page_87]

measurements were certainly not as good as in an experimental station. The crop yields [SOURCE: Page_87]

were estimated based on interviews with the sample farmers, and crosschecked with a [SOURCE: Page_87]

broker in our study area, involved essentially in facilitating the selling and buying of [SOURCE: Page_87]

onions and potatoes. These yields do not depend solely on irrigation practices, but also [SOURCE: Page_87]

on agricultural practices. However, we checked the impact of irrigation practices on [SOURCE: Page_87]

water stress and consequently on crop yields for some selected farms using the CropWat [SOURCE: Page_87]

package. [SOURCE: Page_87]

The logic underlying the choice and use of drip irrigation equipment (farmers’ practices) [SOURCE: Page_87]

was determined during semi-­‐structured interviews using an analytical grid (decision [SOURCE: Page_87]

and motivation for installing drip irrigation, how the equipment was installed, any [SOURCE: Page_87]

adaptations made to the drip system, irrigation practices, maintenance, repairs, etc.), [SOURCE: Page_87]

crosschecked by direct field observations. This enabled us to identify categories of [SOURCE: Page_87]

farmers with a different logic regarding the use of drip irrigation, by combining the [SOURCE: Page_87]

measured performance of the irrigation systems with the insights obtained on irrigation [SOURCE: Page_87]

practices through observations and interviews, using an iterative approach. This [SOURCE: Page_87]

classification helped explain farmers’ practices, and the wider logic of farmers [SOURCE: Page_87]

concerning farming, but also the actual irrigation performance of drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_87]

systems. [SOURCE: Page_87]

We then regrouped the selected farmers in six groups, based on the results of the [SOURCE: Page_87]

irrigation performance as measured, on the irrigation practices observed and on the [SOURCE: Page_87]

logic of farmers. The first group (A) is composed of the large landholders in the region [SOURCE: Page_87]

(three farmers in our sample). Their farms are more than 100 ha in size, and they [SOURCE: Page_87]

practise four different farming systems: orchards, horticulture, livestock, and cereals. In [SOURCE: Page_87]

addition to the land they own, these farmers rent in several plots, which are then farmed [SOURCE: Page_87]

by small-­‐scale farmers on a tenant basis. The second group represents medium-­‐sized [SOURCE: Page_87]

farmers (10-­‐15 ha; three in our sample), which we called reference farmers (B), as their [SOURCE: Page_87]

agricultural and irrigation practices are keenly observed and followed by the majority of [SOURCE: Page_87]

farmers (those from our sample, but more generally most farmers in the study area). [SOURCE: Page_87]

This group of horticultural farmers specialize in the production of onions and potatoes. [SOURCE: Page_87]

The third group represent the small innovators (C$_1$) who may be owners or tenants [SOURCE: Page_87]

(two farmers in our sample). This is a group of small-­‐scale horticultural farmers [SOURCE: Page_87]

(potatoes, onions), who were amongst the first to introduce drip irrigation on small-­‐ scale farms. The learners (C$_2$) are composed of farmers who are either isolated or only [SOURCE: Page_87]

recently started drip irrigation (two in our sample); they have less than 5 ha and grow [SOURCE: Page_87]

onions and potatoes. This is basically a transitory group. The young small-­‐scale [SOURCE: Page_87]

horticultural farmers (D) may be owners, tenants, or lessees; they have less than 10 ha [SOURCE: Page_87]

87 [SOURCE: Page_87]

(nine farmers in our sample) who grow potatoes and onions. This is certainly the largest [SOURCE: Page_88]

group of farmers in the study area practising drip irrigation on horticulture, as drip [SOURCE: Page_88]

irrigation is associated to ‘modern’ farming, and such farmers may obtain access to land [SOURCE: Page_88]

through informal lease agreements. The last group represents the part-­‐time small-­‐scale [SOURCE: Page_88]

horticultural farmers (D’), who have many off-­‐farm activities (three in our sample). [SOURCE: Page_88]

These farmers have less than 5 ha and grow onions and potatoes. [SOURCE: Page_88]

### 3. Results

We first looked at the irrigation performance at the plot level, and then studied the [SOURCE: Page_90]

irrigation practices of farmers in order to explain the wide range of performance [SOURCE: Page_90]

observed. We then analyzed the underlying logic of farmers who operate the drip [SOURCE: Page_90]

irrigation systems. We used the classification of farmers’ groups developed in the [SOURCE: Page_90]

previous section for the presentation of the results. [SOURCE: Page_90]

3.1 Drip irrigation performance [SOURCE: Page_90]

We measured distribution uniformity and irrigation efficiency of drip irrigation systems [SOURCE: Page_90]

in 22 plots for onions and potatoes (table 2). The actual irrigation performance in [SOURCE: Page_90]

farmers’ fields, determined here through two indicators (irrigation efficiency, and [SOURCE: Page_90]

distribution uniformity), was shown to be very heterogeneous. We found a large [SOURCE: Page_90]

variability in the irrigation performance with coefficients of variation of IE and DU of 34% and [SOURCE: Page_90]

37%, respectively. This indicated a strong influence of farmers’ practices on irrigation [SOURCE: Page_90]

performance (Lorite et al. 2004). This was linked to quite generous irrigation supplies, [SOURCE: Page_90]

contrasting with the water saving image of drip irrigation, and to problems of bringing the same [SOURCE: Page_90]

quantities of water across the farm plots, pointing to problems of design or maintenance of drip [SOURCE: Page_90]

irrigation systems. [SOURCE: Page_90]

Irrigation efficiency [SOURCE: Page_90]

Perhaps surprisingly, the vast majority of the farmers over-­‐irrigated their plots (table 2). [SOURCE: Page_90]

The farmers of groups C$_1$ (small innovators) and C$_2$ (learners) applied 2-­‐4 times the [SOURCE: Page_90]

amount of water required by the crop, but even the farmers of groups A (large [SOURCE: Page_90]

landholders) and B (reference farmers) applied 25% to 75% too much water. The [SOURCE: Page_90]

farmers of group D were shown to irrigate the closest to the calculated irrigation water [SOURCE: Page_90]

requirements. Five farmers were within 10% of the required irrigation water [SOURCE: Page_90]

requirements. There are two main reasons for the very high volumes applied. Firstly, [SOURCE: Page_90]

some farmers were trying to compensate for the poor distribution uniformity of their [SOURCE: Page_90]

irrigation systems by applying more water. This was especially the case for the large [SOURCE: Page_90]

landholders and the learners. Secondly, some farmers who had drip irrigation systems [SOURCE: Page_90]

with good distribution uniformity (for example, the reference farmers) applied more [SOURCE: Page_90]

water in an effort to obtain good yields. They allowed a (large) safety margin because [SOURCE: Page_90]

they wanted to be sure that whatever happened, their crop would not suffer from water [SOURCE: Page_90]

stress. Their soils were well-­‐drained, and while over-­‐irrigation would not increase their [SOURCE: Page_90]

yields, too much irrigation water would not affect their yields adversely either. Finally, [SOURCE: Page_90]

among the farmers who aimed for high yields, but who had problems with their [SOURCE: Page_90]

equipment (low DUs), two applied more water for the two above reasons combined. [SOURCE: Page_90]

Distribution uniformity [SOURCE: Page_90]

There was a wide range in the distribution uniformity measured (table 2). Reference [SOURCE: Page_90]

farmers (B) and young horticultural farmers (D) registered fairly good distribution [SOURCE: Page_90]

uniformities, considering that they were measured for field conditions. Six irrigation [SOURCE: Page_90]

systems of groups B and D had a DU of between 80% and 90%, which can be considered [SOURCE: Page_90]

90 [SOURCE: Page_90]

as reasonably good, even though in this case cleaning of the system is generally [SOURCE: Page_91]

recommended as there may be problems of clogging (Pénadille, 1998). These systems [SOURCE: Page_91]

were installed between 1998 and 2008, but had been well maintained by farmers (daily [SOURCE: Page_91]

maintenance, but also replacement of faulty equipment), as these farmers consider drip [SOURCE: Page_91]

irrigation is a way to achieve good yields. Only one irrigation system had more than 90% [SOURCE: Page_91]

distribution uniformity, which is generally considered to be excellent (Keller and [SOURCE: Page_91]

Karmeli, 19747). This was a recent system, installed in 2010 by a local fitter, who [SOURCE: Page_91]

happened to be the uncle of the farmer concerned and is reputed in the region as a good [SOURCE: Page_91]

fitter with a great experience in installing drip irrigation. In addition, we observed that [SOURCE: Page_91]

the farmer undertook maintenance daily (checking the condition of the drip tubing and [SOURCE: Page_91]

cleaning the filters). The remaining 15 drip irrigation systems had a much poorer DU [SOURCE: Page_91]

(average 51%), registered for all the drip irrigation systems of the large landholders (A), [SOURCE: Page_91]

as well as for the small innovators (C$_1$), the learners (C$_2$), the part-­‐time farmers (D’), but [SOURCE: Page_91]

also in a minority of the young horticultural farmers (D). Three systems even had a DU [SOURCE: Page_91]

of between 12% and 30%, two belonged to the large landholders and one to a part-­‐time [SOURCE: Page_91]

farmer. These systems were installed between 1999 and 2010 (table 2). Consequently, [SOURCE: Page_91]

the age of the systems is not decisive in explaining the differences in DU observed, [SOURCE: Page_91]

contrary to our initial hypothesis. [SOURCE: Page_91]

7 Official Moroccan standards (2007) also refer to these authors, when they advise that drip irrigation equipment should guarantee a DU of 90%. [SOURCE: Page_91]

* A ‘complete’ drip irrigation system refers to a system having the following components: a pump, filters, [SOURCE: Page_93]

fertigation unit, main line, laterals, and drippers. None of the systems dispose of a water meter or a [SOURCE: Page_93]

pressure regulator. [SOURCE: Page_93]

** The second small innovator is a lessee, and uses a mobile drip irrigation system. In case of a problem [SOURCE: Page_93]

with the landowner, he can remove the system and fit it on another plot. [SOURCE: Page_93]

Four main problems may explain the poor DUs. Firstly, some of the irrigation systems [SOURCE: Page_93]

had design problems, especially in the case of undulating land, leading to differences in [SOURCE: Page_93]

pressure in the system. In the absence of pressure-­‐compensating drippers, these [SOURCE: Page_93]

pressure differences would lead to differences in discharge. Some farmers (1 small [SOURCE: Page_93]

innovator, 1 learner) installed systems with multiple valves for different irrigation [SOURCE: Page_93]

blocks in order to deal with this problem. However, the measured values of DU remain [SOURCE: Page_93]

poor (73 and 43%, respectively; table 2). Secondly, in some of these systems (3) the [SOURCE: Page_93]

filter system was incomplete (table 2). Either there was only a sand filter, but no disk [SOURCE: Page_93]

filter, or there was no filter at all, leading to clogging of emitters. The absence of filters [SOURCE: Page_93]

can be explained by the lack of means of some small-­‐scale farmers, but most often by the [SOURCE: Page_93]

fact that some farmers simply do not see the point of having such filter systems, mostly [SOURCE: Page_93]

in the case of learners. When the farmers become aware of clogging of emitters, they try [SOURCE: Page_93]

to remedy the problem with acid, but also evolve gradually towards more complete drip [SOURCE: Page_93]

irrigation systems, especially the young horticultural farmers (table 2). Thirdly, there [SOURCE: Page_93]

was a general problem to adequately maintain the drip irrigation systems. Either the [SOURCE: Page_93]

owner was occupied elsewhere (the case of the part-­‐time farmers), or irrigation was [SOURCE: Page_93]

performed by labourers (for instance in the case of the large landholders; table 2), for [SOURCE: Page_93]

whom daily maintenance may be tedious, especially on large farms, and lack of [SOURCE: Page_93]

maintenance does not necessarily have immediate (visible) consequences, but can [SOURCE: Page_93]

gradually reduce distribution uniformity. Fourthly, a small number of farmers did not [SOURCE: Page_93]

replace faulty equipment (perforated tubing, filter systems) in time (table 2). This was [SOURCE: Page_93]

mostly the case of bigger land lords, who received subsidies for the purchase of their [SOURCE: Page_93]

equipment, or farmers who are mostly absent (part-­‐time farmers; table 2) or may even [SOURCE: Page_93]

envisage abandoning a particular plot. [SOURCE: Page_93]

3.2 Farmers’ irrigation practices [SOURCE: Page_93]

The premise of the paper is that farmers’ irrigation practices do not necessarily target [SOURCE: Page_93]

“perfect” irrigation in the sense hoped by engineers. Overall, most farmers over-­‐ irrigated, but on many plots we observed periods of under-­‐irrigation (water stress), [SOURCE: Page_93]

which suggests that the irrigation schedules have scope for improvement to better [SOURCE: Page_93]

match crop water demand and water application. Our observations revealed that the [SOURCE: Page_93]

farmers’ irrigation practices were linked to i. the way farmers perceive the impact of [SOURCE: Page_93]

irrigation on yields, ii. the quality of the irrigation equipment, and iii. constraints on [SOURCE: Page_93]

access to water. [SOURCE: Page_93]

Over-­‐irrigation to achieve optimal yields [SOURCE: Page_93]

The reference farmers (B) aimed to achieve optimal horticultural yields, and generally [SOURCE: Page_93]

over-­‐irrigated to ensure the plants would not suffer from water stress. Table 2 shows [SOURCE: Page_93]

93 [SOURCE: Page_93]

that these farmers obtained the best yields of the sample farmers, i.e. 60-­‐70 T/ha for [SOURCE: Page_94]

potatoes and up to 90 T/ha for onions. In the study area, it is generally considered that [SOURCE: Page_94]

the maximum yield for potatoes is around 80 T/ha, and for onions up to 120 T/ha. The [SOURCE: Page_94]

reference farmers invested in several wells, and had sufficient capacity to deliver water [SOURCE: Page_94]

to all their plots, even during summer. They installed drip irrigation to be able to supply [SOURCE: Page_94]

enough water to all their plants. For such farmers, the cost of irrigation is relatively low [SOURCE: Page_94]

compared to the revenues generated by horticulture, and they did not consider it a [SOURCE: Page_94]

problem to provide 2-­‐3 times the volume of water required, as long as problems of [SOURCE: Page_94]

drainage would not affect their agricultural production. [SOURCE: Page_94]

This was the case for Fatima, who grew horticultural crops on her 10 ha farm, and had [SOURCE: Page_94]

three inter-­‐connected wells. Her objective was clearly to obtain excellent yields, as [SOURCE: Page_94]

confirmed by the labourer in charge of irrigation on her farm: “To be competitive on the [SOURCE: Page_94]

market, you need to have very good yields, and most of all, good quality products. To [SOURCE: Page_94]

achieve this, you need to irrigate well. From the month of June onwards, we increase the [SOURCE: Page_94]

irrigation volume to be sure of obtaining good calibre vegetables and excellent yields” [SOURCE: Page_94]

(Mohamed, 30 years old, 2011). This is how Fatima obtained a yield of 90 tons per ha on [SOURCE: Page_94]

the sample plot (onions), which was the highest yield obtained amongst the sample [SOURCE: Page_94]

farmers (table 2). [SOURCE: Page_94]

Since there were no water constraints on her farm, and Fatima’s equipment was well [SOURCE: Page_94]

maintained (DU=89%), in June, irrigation frequency was increased from once a week to [SOURCE: Page_94]

every second day, and the volume increased from 10.7 mm to 64 mm per week. On one [SOURCE: Page_94]

plot of onions, a total volume of 757 mm was applied, representing 1.64 times net water [SOURCE: Page_94]

requirements (figure 1). [SOURCE: Page_94]

94 [SOURCE: Page_94]

Over-­‐irrigation to compensate for poor quality installations [SOURCE: Page_95]

To obtain reasonable yields, the large landholders (A), small innovators (C$_1$), learners [SOURCE: Page_95]

(C$_2$) and part-­‐time farmers (D’) compensate for poor quality equipment (see table 2) by [SOURCE: Page_95]

over-­‐irrigating. Contrary to our expectations, this generally did not concern only the [SOURCE: Page_95]

small-­‐scale farmers, our hypothesis being that they would lack the means to invest in [SOURCE: Page_95]

good quality equipment. Instead it concerned mainly the large landholders, which [SOURCE: Page_95]

delegated irrigation and maintenance to labourers, and were rarely present in the field [SOURCE: Page_95]

to monitor irrigation performance. This was the case of Yacine, whose equipment had [SOURCE: Page_95]

very poor distribution uniformity (12%), and who applied 217% of net irrigation water [SOURCE: Page_95]

requirements in order to ensure a yield of 40 tons per ha of potato, which is considered [SOURCE: Page_95]

half of the maximum yield recorded in the region (table 2). [SOURCE: Page_95]

However, we observed periods of under-­‐irrigation [SOURCE: Page_95]

Even though, when we checked the total irrigation volumes over an agricultural season, [SOURCE: Page_95]

we noted that almost all of the farmers over-­‐irrigated, our observations also revealed [SOURCE: Page_95]

the existence of periods when farmers underestimated irrigation requirements. This [SOURCE: Page_95]

was the case for most of the young horticultural farmers (D) and the part-­‐time farmers [SOURCE: Page_95]

(D’). In the case of onions, these farmers obtained 50-­‐70 tons per ha (with an average of [SOURCE: Page_95]

58 tons per ha), which was 25-­‐45% less than the best yield obtained by one of the [SOURCE: Page_95]

reference farmers (table 2). However, even the reference farmers (B) were shown to [SOURCE: Page_95]

under-­‐irrigate at times. In the case of Fatima (fig. 1), we calculated a very mild water [SOURCE: Page_95]

stress in the development stage of the crop, leading to a calculated yield reduction of [SOURCE: Page_95]

0.7% on the sample plot (loam). [SOURCE: Page_95]

For most farmers of the other categories, these periods of under-­‐irrigation were even [SOURCE: Page_95]

more pronounced. Monim, one of the young horticultural farmers (D), applied a total [SOURCE: Page_95]

volume of 713 mm to his onion crop, 25% more than irrigation water requirements on [SOURCE: Page_95]

his sample plot (loam). However, in April-­‐May he under-­‐irrigated (compared to net [SOURCE: Page_95]

irrigation water requirements), as he believed the onion plants did not yet require much [SOURCE: Page_95]

water (figure 2). He attributed this both to the size of the (young) plants, and to the [SOURCE: Page_95]

climatic conditions (relatively cold). For Monim, the true irrigation season had not yet [SOURCE: Page_95]

started. However, a comparison with the net water requirements revealed that [SOURCE: Page_95]

insufficient irrigation water was applied at times, leading to water stress in the [SOURCE: Page_95]

development-­‐ and mid-­‐stages of the crop, and in turn, to a calculated yield reduction of [SOURCE: Page_95]

11,5%. [SOURCE: Page_95]

Figure 2 Irrigation applications compared to net irrigation water requirements [SOURCE: Page_96]

(both in mm) on a plot of onions on Monim’s farm [SOURCE: Page_96]

This phenomenon concerned most farmers. They underestimated irrigation water [SOURCE: Page_96]

requirements at the beginning of the season. From May, they increased irrigation [SOURCE: Page_96]

frequency and volumes in order to obtain good yields, but also to compensate for the [SOURCE: Page_96]

problems they had with the hydraulic performance of their drip systems (poor DUs). [SOURCE: Page_96]

In some cases, the irrigation practices were constrained by access to water [SOURCE: Page_96]

Even in the case of groundwater irrigation with private wells, it is important to take [SOURCE: Page_96]

water availability into account to explain irrigation practices. Five farms had water [SOURCE: Page_96]

constrains and farmers could not irrigate the entire plot according to the crop water [SOURCE: Page_96]

requirements, especially during the summer months during peak crop water [SOURCE: Page_96]

requirements. These farmers (all large landholders and two farms belonging to young [SOURCE: Page_96]

horticultural farmers) had a problem of well capacity. At these farms the continuous [SOURCE: Page_96]

flow, based on the number of wells, well capacity and surface of irrigated land, ranged [SOURCE: Page_96]

from 0.20 to 1.00 l s-­‐1 ha-­‐1. Considering a peak demand of 8 mm d-­‐1 and a potential [SOURCE: Page_96]

irrigation efficiency of 90%, the peak demand should be at least 1,03 l s-­‐1 ha-­‐1. These five [SOURCE: Page_96]

farmers had simply not enough water for their plots and accepted sub-­‐optimal yields [SOURCE: Page_96]

rather than diminishing the irrigated area. [SOURCE: Page_96]

On the other farms, farmers had theoretically sufficient discharge capacity of wells to [SOURCE: Page_96]

increase the duration or the frequency of irrigation when water requirements increased [SOURCE: Page_96]

(from June onwards). However, on some farms the yield of wells, i.e. the ability to [SOURCE: Page_96]

deliver the peak discharge over a longer period of time, constituted also a problem for [SOURCE: Page_96]

some farmers, especially during the summer. Only farmers with sufficient means were [SOURCE: Page_96]

120 [SOURCE: Page_96]

100 [SOURCE: Page_96]

80 [SOURCE: Page_96]

60 [SOURCE: Page_96]

40 [SOURCE: Page_96]

20 [SOURCE: Page_96]

0 [SOURCE: Page_96]

96 [SOURCE: Page_96]

able to install additional wells on the farm. Those farmers who did not have such means [SOURCE: Page_97]

thus had to reorganize how water was distributed within the farm, and even within a [SOURCE: Page_97]

plot. These farmers used two main strategies. [SOURCE: Page_97]

• Irrigating a plot in different turns during the day [SOURCE: Page_97]

One of the farmers of the learners’ group, who owns 3 ha with only one well (45 m in [SOURCE: Page_97]

depth), did not have sufficient discharge during the summer, and divided his plot into [SOURCE: Page_97]

three irrigation blocks. His well would run dry after only a few hours of functioning. To [SOURCE: Page_97]

solve this problem, he irrigated two blocks in the morning, and one block in the [SOURCE: Page_97]

afternoon. In this way, he compensated for the water shortage by increasing the [SOURCE: Page_97]

duration of irrigation in the plot as a whole, and thus a longer presence in the field. He [SOURCE: Page_97]

applied more water to the plot (224% of the water requirements) than required. [SOURCE: Page_97]

Interestingly, the water constraint did not seem to be sufficiently problematic for him to [SOURCE: Page_97]

either deal with the yield problem of his well (e.g. digging a second well, increasing the [SOURCE: Page_97]

discharge of the existing pump), or improving the poor irrigation performance of his [SOURCE: Page_97]

irrigation system (DU of 43%). [SOURCE: Page_97]

• Increasing the frequency of irrigation [SOURCE: Page_97]

Several farmers (young horticultural farmers, learners and part-­‐time farmers) were not [SOURCE: Page_97]

able to increase the volume of a single irrigation during the period of peak water [SOURCE: Page_97]

requirements in summer, as their well ran dry. To deal with the problem, they increased [SOURCE: Page_97]

the frequency of irrigation. Monim -­‐one of young horticultural farmers-­‐ who cultivates 3 [SOURCE: Page_97]

ha of onions, started the irrigation season by irrigating every week. From the end of May, [SOURCE: Page_97]

he increased the frequency, to every two days. In addition, he took great care of his [SOURCE: Page_97]

irrigation equipment (installed in 2008), and had good distribution uniformity (85.6%). [SOURCE: Page_97]

It is interesting to note that this farmer, like many farmers in the area, used gravity [SOURCE: Page_97]

irrigation (small basins) to “establish” the crop. However, this initial application was not [SOURCE: Page_97]

sufficient to avoid plant water stress, as he under-­‐irrigated during the first part of the [SOURCE: Page_97]

season (April to mid-­‐May). Our observations of the total irrigation volume applied [SOURCE: Page_97]

during the season, revealed that he over-­‐irrigated, despite the problems he faced with [SOURCE: Page_97]

his well (IE of 80%). [SOURCE: Page_97]

By reducing the duration of irrigation, the farmer “saves” water [SOURCE: Page_97]

All farmers are well aware of the concept of saving water, which has been promoted by [SOURCE: Page_97]

the state. However, in their mind, the concept is less linked to the volume of water used [SOURCE: Page_97]

for irrigation, but rather to the duration of irrigation, which was dramatically reduced [SOURCE: Page_97]

when drip irrigation was introduced: [SOURCE: Page_97]

“Drip irrigation is good. It is a water saving technique. Before, I used to take four days to [SOURCE: Page_97]

irrigate 1 ha; with drip irrigation one day is sufficient to irrigate my plot” (Mohamed, 26 [SOURCE: Page_97]

years old, 2011). [SOURCE: Page_97]

Indeed, there were no water meters in 21 out of 22 drip irrigation systems, and none of [SOURCE: Page_97]

the farmers knew how much water they applied, or how much water they could save. [SOURCE: Page_97]

97 [SOURCE: Page_97]

3.3 Logic behind farmers adopting and using drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_98]

The results of the previous section showed that farmers’ practices are a determining [SOURCE: Page_98]

factor in the performance of drip irrigation systems (efficiency), along with the [SOURCE: Page_98]

condition of the equipment (DU), which in turn depends on the design and maintenance [SOURCE: Page_98]

of the system. However, it is necessary to go one step further in the analysis by [SOURCE: Page_98]

understanding the social context and the logic behind the farmers’ (technical) irrigation [SOURCE: Page_98]

practices. In this section, we identify different categories of farmers based on their [SOURCE: Page_98]

irrigation practices, as well as the logic behind their practices (table 3, fig. 3). We show [SOURCE: Page_98]

that hydraulic performance reflects differences between farmers in how they conceive [SOURCE: Page_98]

and practice agriculture. [SOURCE: Page_98]

Table 3. The underlying logic of different groups of farmers operating drip [SOURCE: Page_98]

irrigation systems. [SOURCE: Page_98]

Group Prevailing logic of farmers [SOURCE: Page_98]

with respect to drip [SOURCE: Page_98]

irrigation [SOURCE: Page_98]

Large landholders (A) ‘Landlord’ status [SOURCE: Page_98]

Increasing the size of the farm [SOURCE: Page_98]

Reference farmers (B) Excellency [SOURCE: Page_98]

Small innovators (C$_1$) ‘Modern farmer’ status [SOURCE: Page_98]

Being a drip irrigation expert [SOURCE: Page_98]

Learners (C$_2$) Aiming for intensification [SOURCE: Page_98]

Young horticultural farmers Solving technical constraints [SOURCE: Page_98]

(D) Intensification [SOURCE: Page_98]

Part-­‐time farmers (D’) Ease of use [SOURCE: Page_98]

Large landholders: every new innovation is first adopted by these farmers (Group [SOURCE: Page_98]

A) [SOURCE: Page_98]

The large landholders have a wide social and professional network, meaning they are [SOURCE: Page_98]

always informed about the latest technology. They are generally office bearers in the [SOURCE: Page_98]

farmers’ organizations, for example, the regional dairy cooperative. In the mid-­‐1980s, [SOURCE: Page_98]

several companies from Casablanca (importers of drip equipment) came to the Saiss [SOURCE: Page_98]

region and encouraged these large landholders to start drip irrigation on their farms on [SOURCE: Page_98]

a trial basis. Often, the equipment was installed on small plots (1-­‐2 ha) free of charge. [SOURCE: Page_98]

For these landholders, being a “big” farmer is an identity that requires them to stay [SOURCE: Page_98]

informed about innovations, and to be the first to adopt new technologies. [SOURCE: Page_98]

“At the time, a company from Casablanca came to see me to suggest drip irrigation. I [SOURCE: Page_98]

remember that they installed it cost free on a 1 ha plot of plum trees. I could not refuse the [SOURCE: Page_98]

98 [SOURCE: Page_98]

offer, it was free of charge, it was an opportunity to discover drip irrigation” (El Haj Driss, [SOURCE: Page_99]

80 years old, 2011). [SOURCE: Page_99]

When the farmers tested the innovation, they were impressed by the high yields [SOURCE: Page_99]

obtained after the introduction of drip irrigation. They consequently progressively [SOURCE: Page_99]

equipped the whole farm with drip irrigation. Later on, the government also started to [SOURCE: Page_99]

show interest in the technology, and this group of farmers often managed to obtain [SOURCE: Page_99]

government subsidies for their irrigation equipment. Today, all their plots are equipped. [SOURCE: Page_99]

They even equip the plots they rent, requiring their tenants to adopt drip irrigation too. [SOURCE: Page_99]

In so doing, they are no longer aiming to maintain their “status”, as today drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_99]

is now being used by a wide range of farmers, and is thus no longer a distinguishing [SOURCE: Page_99]

factor for such large landholders. Instead, their current aim is to increase crop yields, [SOURCE: Page_99]

thereby “rationalizing” their intentions, and to reduce labour requirements. However, [SOURCE: Page_99]

they still want to be informed about innovation, and be the first to try new inventions [SOURCE: Page_99]

(integrated automation systems for drip irrigation, for instance), and thus maintain their [SOURCE: Page_99]

social status. To do so, they mobilize their professional networks, apply to state agencies [SOURCE: Page_99]

for subsidies, and invest their own money. This does not always guarantee success. In [SOURCE: Page_99]

one case, the (fully automated) high-­‐tech system turned out to be unsuitable in the local [SOURCE: Page_99]

conditions: “The first few days after the company had installed the automatic drip [SOURCE: Page_99]

irrigation system, I found it very beautiful, and very sophisticated. But when I started to use [SOURCE: Page_99]

it, I discovered how complicated it was. Especially the fertigation system, and the solenoid [SOURCE: Page_99]

valves; when there is an electricity cut, you can’t irrigate. After a week of use, I replaced the [SOURCE: Page_99]

big fertilizer tub by smaller tubs to make it easier to measure the doses of fertilizer, and [SOURCE: Page_99]

replaced the solenoid valves by manual valves” (Driss, manager of a large-­‐scale farm, 42 [SOURCE: Page_99]

years old, 2011). [SOURCE: Page_99]

The performance of the equipment belonging to the farmers in this group was [SOURCE: Page_99]

surprisingly low, with DUs ranging from 12% to 55% (fig. 3, table 2). This can be [SOURCE: Page_99]

explained by the fact that while these farmers are fascinated by the technology, they are [SOURCE: Page_99]

not involved in the practical use of the equipment, which is handled by labourers or [SOURCE: Page_99]

tenants. These workers do not spend much time on maintenance (renewing the tubes, [SOURCE: Page_99]

cleaning the filters), as it has only a gradual (negative) impact on hydraulic performance. [SOURCE: Page_99]

In addition, the plots are generally quite large, making it difficult to inspect all the drip [SOURCE: Page_99]

tubes. Lastly, these farmers are clearly more interested in increasing the size of their [SOURCE: Page_99]

farm, by renting in or purchasing land, than in intensifying the use of the land they [SOURCE: Page_99]

already own. For all these reasons, the DUs of drip irrigation systems were very poor, [SOURCE: Page_99]

which the farmers compensated for by using large volumes of irrigation water (200% of [SOURCE: Page_99]

net irrigation water requirements, on average). Since these farmers have installed [SOURCE: Page_99]

several often inter-­‐connected wells, supplying sufficient water to compensate for [SOURCE: Page_99]

technical deficiencies is not a problem. [SOURCE: Page_99]

Reference farmers (Group B) [SOURCE: Page_100]

Interestingly, the majority of farmers (those from our sample, but more generally most [SOURCE: Page_100]

farmers in the study area) rarely refer to large landholders as a reference for their [SOURCE: Page_100]

farming and (drip) irrigation practices. Instead, they turn to a small select group of [SOURCE: Page_100]

medium-­‐sized farmers, we called “reference farmers”. This group of horticultural [SOURCE: Page_100]

farmers, aim for excellence (calibre, quality of products, yields). These farmers are keen [SOURCE: Page_100]

to know about any technological advances, but also pay a great deal of attention to new [SOURCE: Page_100]

agricultural and irrigation practices. They often consult specialists for answers to [SOURCE: Page_100]

specific questions related to technology and practices (fertigation, for example), and fix [SOURCE: Page_100]

very clear targets for their production as they aim for specific markets. Like the large [SOURCE: Page_100]

landholders, they hire labourers but they supervise them very closely, and are present [SOURCE: Page_100]

on their farm every day. Their daily presence in the field also means they interact more [SOURCE: Page_100]

with farmers in other groups, who are keen to learn from these reference farmers. [SOURCE: Page_100]

“The first farmer to introduce drip irrigation in this village was “big” Mohamed (group B, [SOURCE: Page_100]

reference farmers). He is known to be a very competent farmer, he is a hard worker! He [SOURCE: Page_100]

spends all his time on his farm! He has even installed a new crushing unit to make olive oil. [SOURCE: Page_100]

He knows all the new techniques, and especially the best seed potatoes. He obtains excellent [SOURCE: Page_100]

yields. Every year, before buying seed potatoes, I ask him for advice on which to buy” [SOURCE: Page_100]

(Taher, 31 years old, 2011, group C$_1$). [SOURCE: Page_100]

Irrigation [SOURCE: Page_100]

100 [SOURCE: Page_100]

This group of farmers introduced drip irrigation from 1996 onwards with the aim of [SOURCE: Page_101]

intensification. They had observed drip irrigation on the large landholders’ farms (group [SOURCE: Page_101]

A), and were aware of its advantages. For them, drip irrigation is part of a larger [SOURCE: Page_101]

intensive farming system. They adopted drip irrigation step-­‐by-­‐step. They first tried it [SOURCE: Page_101]

out in a small plot to gain experience, but also to reduce the cost of investment. [SOURCE: Page_101]

Progressively, they equipped their whole farm. The first plots were equipped by [SOURCE: Page_101]

companies in the vicinity, but later on, they purchased spare parts and installed drip [SOURCE: Page_101]

irrigation in their plots themselves. Too busy on their farm to “waste” their time [SOURCE: Page_101]

negotiating with government administrations, and convinced that their investment [SOURCE: Page_101]

would quickly pay for itself, these farmers equipped their farm without applying for [SOURCE: Page_101]

state subsidies. [SOURCE: Page_101]

These reference farmers consider maintaining the equipment and monitoring irrigation [SOURCE: Page_101]

are very important. For them “good irrigation” implies having excellent irrigation [SOURCE: Page_101]

equipment (DU ranging from 73% to 89%; fig. 3, table 2), and ensuring that their plants [SOURCE: Page_101]

never undergo water stress, as they have a clear production objective and do not want to [SOURCE: Page_101]

take any risks. They generally over-­‐irrigate (164%-­‐196% of net irrigation water [SOURCE: Page_101]

requirements). [SOURCE: Page_101]

Small innovators (group C$_1$) were amongst the first to introduce drip irrigation on [SOURCE: Page_101]

small plots. They acquired know-­‐how about drip irrigation while working as labourers [SOURCE: Page_101]

on large farms. Once they were back on the family farm, they designed a drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_101]

system suited to their own requirements: simple to use, less expensive than high-­‐tech [SOURCE: Page_101]

installations. Taher, for example, worked on a large farm where drip irrigation was [SOURCE: Page_101]

introduced in 2002. He was even involved in installing the irrigation equipment there. [SOURCE: Page_101]

He wanted to understand how drip irrigation worked, as it was the first time in his life [SOURCE: Page_101]

he has seen it. When he returned to his own family farm, and after an excellent [SOURCE: Page_101]

agricultural campaign in 2005, he installed a simplified drip irrigation system on his [SOURCE: Page_101]

own farm (small valves, simplified fertigation system). [SOURCE: Page_101]

……looking for social status [SOURCE: Page_101]

For this group of farmers, drip irrigation is a synonym for a high social status. It is about [SOURCE: Page_101]

being a “modern farmer”. The main motivation for adopting drip irrigation in the first [SOURCE: Page_101]

instance is to achieve this status. Taher used to be a labourer on a large farm. When he [SOURCE: Page_101]

introduced drip irrigation on his family farm, his aim was to put himself on the same [SOURCE: Page_101]

level as large-­‐scale farmers, but also to improve his status in his family environment. His [SOURCE: Page_101]

uncle, with whom he was in conflict over land inheritance, had installed drip irrigation, [SOURCE: Page_101]

and had become a reference in the village: “the day I installed drip irrigation, I was proud [SOURCE: Page_101]

of myself. I finally had drip irrigation, there was no longer any difference between me and [SOURCE: Page_101]

my uncle” (Taher, 31 years old, 2011). [SOURCE: Page_101]

The farmers in this group clearly regard reference farmers (group B) as an example to [SOURCE: Page_101]

be followed. In turn, these small innovators have considerable influence on other small-­‐ scale farmers (group D, for instance). As Taher’s mother said: “the day my son installed [SOURCE: Page_101]

101 [SOURCE: Page_101]

drip irrigation, it was like a wedding party. I invited my neighbours, and I organized a [SOURCE: Page_102]

small party. Oh yes, on that day I was happy, it felt right, now we had drip irrigation like [SOURCE: Page_102]

the large-­‐scale farmers. I showed the drip irrigation system to my neighbours. Afterwards, [SOURCE: Page_102]

everybody wanted to install drip irrigation. Our neighbour Mounir, for example, came over [SOURCE: Page_102]

next day and asked Taher to install drip irrigation on his farm” (mother of Taher, 62 years [SOURCE: Page_102]

old, 2011). [SOURCE: Page_102]

Today, Taher installs drip irrigation systems for farmers, gives advice to friends and [SOURCE: Page_102]

neighbours on the type of equipment to install, and advises his clients on which dealer [SOURCE: Page_102]

or fitter to contact to purchase and install the equipment. This means that farmers in [SOURCE: Page_102]

this group often do not have the time and the energy to spend sufficient time on their [SOURCE: Page_102]

own farm. The performance of the drip systems is relatively poor (DU=55-­‐73), which [SOURCE: Page_102]

they compensate for by over-­‐irrigating, as they want to obtain the same yields as the [SOURCE: Page_102]

reference farmers (IE=25 and 36%, respectively; table 2). [SOURCE: Page_102]

The second example is Khalid, who worked as a labourer on some large-­‐scale farms in [SOURCE: Page_102]

France, where he discovered and used drip irrigation. When he returned to Morocco in [SOURCE: Page_102]

2006, he decided to install drip irrigation on the family farm: [SOURCE: Page_102]

“When I returned home from France, I immediately installed drip irrigation. At the time, [SOURCE: Page_102]

drip irrigation was not very common in the village. It was a way to show the villagers that I [SOURCE: Page_102]

came back with a certain know-­‐how from France, and that this know-­‐how related to [SOURCE: Page_102]

something important: drip irrigation. I installed it myself. And since then, everybody talks [SOURCE: Page_102]

about me as somebody who went abroad, and came back with something. He didn’t come [SOURCE: Page_102]

back empty handed” (Khalid, 60 years old, 2011). [SOURCE: Page_102]

In contrast to the first farmer, Khalid’s logic changed with time. While initially drip [SOURCE: Page_102]

irrigation was mainly a status symbol, today his aim is to increase his crop yields. This is [SOURCE: Page_102]

also linked to the fact that his son (26 years old) has taken over most of the management [SOURCE: Page_102]

of the farm. For example, unlike the first farmer, they renew their drip tubes, and are [SOURCE: Page_102]

very careful to maintain the equipment to ensure the irrigation system continues to [SOURCE: Page_102]

function properly. Khalid is now part of group D (DU=82%; IE=97%). [SOURCE: Page_102]

The small-­‐scale horticultural farmers: Learners (C$_2$), Young horticultural farmers [SOURCE: Page_102]

(D), Part-­‐time farmers (D’) [SOURCE: Page_102]

These farmers have less than 5 ha, and may be owners, tenants, or lessees. They adopted [SOURCE: Page_102]

drip irrigation from 2006 onwards. Adoption was facilitated by three concomitant [SOURCE: Page_102]

phenomena: 1) they learned about drip irrigation as labourers on large or medium-­‐scale [SOURCE: Page_102]

farms (groups A and B), 2) pioneer horticulturalists adopted drip irrigation on small-­‐ scale farms in their village, thus showing the feasibility of using drip irrigation on such [SOURCE: Page_102]

farms (group C$_1$), and 3) local expertise was developed concerning the sale and [SOURCE: Page_102]

installation of drip irrigation equipment. This local support sector proposed cheaper [SOURCE: Page_102]

equipment that was simpler to use, and suitable for the specific requirements of the [SOURCE: Page_102]

farms. For example, on undulating plots or farms with problems of well discharge, they [SOURCE: Page_102]

102 [SOURCE: Page_102]

proposed drip tubes with individual valves, enabling the farmer to adjust the pressure as [SOURCE: Page_103]

required, and irrigate line-­‐by-­‐line. Certain farmers initially hesitated, but were [SOURCE: Page_103]

convinced by the results obtained by neighbouring farmers. These farmers were not [SOURCE: Page_103]

only able to obtain good onion and potato yields, but also gained in terms of the number [SOURCE: Page_103]

of labourers required and the duration of irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_103]

“I installed drip irrigation when my neighbour Taher installed it on his plot. He explained [SOURCE: Page_103]

what to do, and told me whom to contact. Finally, it is easy to have drip irrigation. When I [SOURCE: Page_103]

installed it, I used it to grow onions, as everybody said that onions grow well with drip [SOURCE: Page_103]

irrigation and give excellent yields” (Mounir, 27 years old, 2011). [SOURCE: Page_103]

In these three groups, we were given a range of different reasons for introducing drip [SOURCE: Page_103]

irrigation. These reasons did not (or rarely) relate to improving social status, but to [SOURCE: Page_103]

specific technical objectives (increasing agricultural production, obtaining a better [SOURCE: Page_103]

quality product), and in response to specific constraints. In a way, drip irrigation had [SOURCE: Page_103]

been demystified. The constraints related to water shortage, the difficulty of finding [SOURCE: Page_103]

labourers, or the kind of topography that makes gravity irrigation difficult or even [SOURCE: Page_103]

impossible. Drip irrigation thus allowed farmers to extend their irrigated area: “I decided [SOURCE: Page_103]

to install drip irrigation when I saw it at my neighbour’s, who does not have sufficient [SOURCE: Page_103]

water in his well. With drip irrigation, he could irrigate his entire plot. I contacted the son [SOURCE: Page_103]

of a friend at the milk cooperative, who suggested I install a drip system with individual [SOURCE: Page_103]

valves. This system was not very expensive, and was suitable for the conditions of my land; [SOURCE: Page_103]

There is not enough water in my well, and my farm plot is on a small hill” (Younes, 62 [SOURCE: Page_103]

years old, 2011). [SOURCE: Page_103]

Today, there is a clear distinction between the three different groups of small-­‐scale [SOURCE: Page_103]

horticultural farmers. The farmers in group C$_2$ are “learners”. Their equipment is [SOURCE: Page_103]

generally of poor quality, and they face many problems in their agricultural and [SOURCE: Page_103]

irrigation practices. They are either isolated (limited social network), or only recently [SOURCE: Page_103]

started drip irrigation. Group C$_2$ is thus a temporary category, which farmers will leave [SOURCE: Page_103]

after 1-­‐2 irrigation seasons, most likely for group D (or D’). [SOURCE: Page_103]

Group D comprises young farmers aiming for intensive horticultural production. For [SOURCE: Page_103]

them, drip irrigation is part and parcel of ‘modern’ agriculture and an obvious choice [SOURCE: Page_103]

that enables them to deal with specific constraints (labour, precise application of inputs, [SOURCE: Page_103]

etc.). They closely observe the reference farmers (group B), and manage to closely follow [SOURCE: Page_103]

their farming and irrigation practices. They also take advantage of the advice and [SOURCE: Page_103]

support of local fitters. These (young) farmers manage the technology well (installation [SOURCE: Page_103]

and maintenance). Once these small-­‐scale farmers have used drip irrigation for 2-­‐3 [SOURCE: Page_103]

years, they may change their irrigation equipment in order to be “be more efficient and [SOURCE: Page_103]

produce more”, thereby also improving the distribution uniformity. Karim, for example, [SOURCE: Page_103]

adopted drip irrigation to deal with the problem of insufficient water in his well. In [SOURCE: Page_103]

2010, he installed cheap, mobile equipment with individual valves, without fertigation [SOURCE: Page_103]

103 [SOURCE: Page_103]

or filter systems. Today, he is in the process of installing a complete drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_104]

package on his entire farm, in order to obtain better yields. He is about to change from [SOURCE: Page_104]

group D to group B (fig 4). [SOURCE: Page_104]

The farmers in group D’ are only on their farm part of the time, as they have off-­‐farm [SOURCE: Page_104]

activities. Their limited presence in the field is reflected in the state of their drip [SOURCE: Page_104]

irrigation systems, which are generally in bad condition (clogging, poor distribution [SOURCE: Page_104]

uniformity; table 2). While in their interviews they talked about having the same [SOURCE: Page_104]

intensification objectives as the farmers in group B, in practice, their irrigation and [SOURCE: Page_104]

agricultural performance never matched their ambitions. Drip irrigation is often a way [SOURCE: Page_104]

for them to reduce their presence on the farm and a technology with a certain ease of [SOURCE: Page_104]

use. [SOURCE: Page_104]

3.4 [SOURCE: Page_104]

Interactions between logics, and groups, evolving logics [SOURCE: Page_104]

Our results show that the (prevailing) logic behind farmers’ adoption and use of drip [SOURCE: Page_104]

irrigation differs, and that these differences have an impact on the quality of the [SOURCE: Page_104]

irrigation equipment, the irrigation practices and the maintenance of the equipment, [SOURCE: Page_104]

and hence on irrigation performance itself. Of course, no farmer (nor any group) acts on [SOURCE: Page_104]

only one logic, and there is often a combination of mutually reinforcing rationalities at [SOURCE: Page_104]

play. However, our discussions with farmers, as well as our field observations, revealed [SOURCE: Page_104]

that the prevailing logic differed significantly in the different categories of farmers. [SOURCE: Page_104]

Interestingly, the farmers themselves identified different categories of irrigation [SOURCE: Page_104]

practices, and could easily relate to the categories we identified. [SOURCE: Page_104]

It was also interesting to note that the logic of farmers often changes once they start to [SOURCE: Page_104]

use the drip irrigation system. Status is still relatively important, but less so since many [SOURCE: Page_104]

farmers have adopted drip irrigation in the meantime. After 2-­‐3 years of use, having [SOURCE: Page_104]

improved their income, almost all farmers display economic logic, aiming to intensify [SOURCE: Page_104]

their agricultural production. For small-­‐scale farmers, this means intensifying [SOURCE: Page_104]

production on their small plots. For large-­‐scale farmers, this generally means increasing [SOURCE: Page_104]

the size of the farm, while aiming at reasonably high yields, but without achieving the [SOURCE: Page_104]

results of small-­‐scale farmers. During this process, farmers often renew their irrigation [SOURCE: Page_104]

equipment. While small-­‐scale farmers at first buy cheap simple equipment (with a [SOURCE: Page_104]

simplified filter system, for instance), after 2-­‐3 years of use, they change to more [SOURCE: Page_104]

complete irrigation systems. They not only have obtained the means to invest, but their [SOURCE: Page_104]

ambitions have also changed. [SOURCE: Page_104]

As mentioned above, there are many interactions between the different groups with [SOURCE: Page_104]

exchanges of knowledge, know-­‐how, and practices, as farmers closely observed not only [SOURCE: Page_104]

the farmers in their own group, but also those in other groups (fig. 4). These interactions [SOURCE: Page_104]

influenced the logic of farmers, gave them ideas, which may lead to new investments in [SOURCE: Page_104]

irrigation equipment, and to changing practices. We present two types of interactions in [SOURCE: Page_104]

104 [SOURCE: Page_104]

figure 4. Firstly, farmers of a particular group observe farmers’ practices of other [SOURCE: Page_105]

groups, which thus serve as role models (dotted arrow). However, in some cases these [SOURCE: Page_105]

farmers could not implement such practices, due to insufficient means or other [SOURCE: Page_105]

constraints, as mentioned in fig. 4. This explains their current practices. Secondly, some [SOURCE: Page_105]

farmers who observed certain practices of other farmers’ groups adapted them to their [SOURCE: Page_105]

situation, and thus changed their practices (plain arrow). [SOURCE: Page_105]

Figure 4 Interactions between the different groups of farmers related to drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_105]

and agricultural practices [SOURCE: Page_105]

Farmers may change category, but not all manage to catch up with other groups [SOURCE: Page_105]

The dynamics related to horticulture and drip irrigation can change rapidly. Farmers [SOURCE: Page_105]

may move from one group to another as their logic changes or when they are able to [SOURCE: Page_105]

invest more, for example in new equipment, or by improving their practices. In most [SOURCE: Page_105]

cases, the farmers in group B (medium-­‐sized farms) are the main reference, especially [SOURCE: Page_105]

for those in group D, who are all small-­‐scale farmers (fig 4). Group D farmers imitate the [SOURCE: Page_105]

practices of the reference farmers, but their equipment is not as good (lower DU; table [SOURCE: Page_105]

2), and they have less access to water, as they do not have the means to dig additional [SOURCE: Page_105]

wells. This leads to lower yields, and thus lower revenues, and a continuing difference [SOURCE: Page_105]

between these groups. “There is no big difference between me and Mohamed (group B, [SOURCE: Page_105]

105 [SOURCE: Page_105]

reference farmers), I have got good equipment. But I do not have enough water, otherwise I [SOURCE: Page_106]

would just be like him” (Monîm, 26 years, 2011). Interestingly, the vast majority of the [SOURCE: Page_106]

small-­‐scale farmers (C$_2$, D) did not refer to the large landholders (group A) as a source [SOURCE: Page_106]

of inspiration, even though some had worked on their farms and learned about drip [SOURCE: Page_106]

irrigation there. The (poor) performance of the drip irrigation systems we observed on [SOURCE: Page_106]

these big farms, along with erratic irrigation and maintenance, tended to confirm the [SOURCE: Page_106]

views of these small-­‐scale farmers. However, the small innovators (C$_1$) continued to [SOURCE: Page_106]

observe changes in irrigation equipment in the farms of large landholders. Through their [SOURCE: Page_106]

close links with labourers and farm managers who work there, they obtained [SOURCE: Page_106]

information more easily than on the farms of reference farmers. [SOURCE: Page_106]

Other small-­‐scale farmers (C$_1$, D) also aimed to reproduce the agricultural achievements [SOURCE: Page_106]

of the reference farmers (group B). However, they faced two types of obstacles. Firstly, [SOURCE: Page_106]

their equipment did not perform as well (table 2, group C$_1$). Secondly, some farmers had [SOURCE: Page_106]

problems with their irrigation practices. Some may have had to face a general shortage [SOURCE: Page_106]

of water in their well that limited their irrigation capacity, but more often they had [SOURCE: Page_106]

difficulty in correctly estimating the water requirements of the plants they cultivated. [SOURCE: Page_106]

Similar problems affected their agricultural practices, which impacts on yields and on [SOURCE: Page_106]

the quality of the products. As the horticultural market becomes more competitive, the [SOURCE: Page_106]

agronomic performance (yields, product quality) is considered more and more [SOURCE: Page_106]

important: “this year, I sold my onions to Marjane (supermarket) in Casablanca for 6 [SOURCE: Page_106]

dirhams per kilo, better than Mohamed (group B, reference farmers), and next year, I hope [SOURCE: Page_106]

to have the same yields as he has, so that I can sell all of it to Marjane” (Taher, 32 years [SOURCE: Page_106]

old, 2012). [SOURCE: Page_106]

The irrigation practices of farmers in group C$_2$ were problematic, mainly due to their [SOURCE: Page_106]

lack of experience in the use of drip irrigation, while some lacked interest in their [SOURCE: Page_106]

agricultural activities. With time, some of these farmers may reach the same hydraulic [SOURCE: Page_106]

performance as those in group D, but many have structural problems that would be [SOURCE: Page_106]

difficult to overcome. While they claimed their logic was the same logic as that of [SOURCE: Page_106]

farmers in group D, many of them have off-­‐farm activities, and simply do not have the [SOURCE: Page_106]

time, or even the interest in improving their practices. For them, drip irrigation is a [SOURCE: Page_106]

means to reduce labour requirements, and thus the need for their presence on the plot. [SOURCE: Page_106]

### 4. Discussion: Beyond water saving, putting drip irrigation performance in a wider

perspective [SOURCE: Page_106]

In recent years, irrigation engineers have made a major effort to clarify the notion of [SOURCE: Page_106]

irrigation performance, and to define appropriate indicators (Burt et al. 1997). Our [SOURCE: Page_106]

study showed that these indicators are relatively easy to apply, even though they [SOURCE: Page_106]

require considerable effort to collect data, particularly field data on irrigation [SOURCE: Page_106]

applications. These indicators were also helpful to distinguish between problems [SOURCE: Page_106]

affecting irrigation equipment (DU), and irrigation practices (IE). However, our study [SOURCE: Page_106]

also showed that farmers define irrigation performance in different ways, relating them [SOURCE: Page_106]

106 [SOURCE: Page_106]

to other objectives than ‘just’ water. Leeuwis and van den Ban (2004) provide four sets [SOURCE: Page_107]

of variables to understand the logic underlying individual (irrigation) practices. Firstly, [SOURCE: Page_107]

farmers rarely link the notion of “good irrigation” with efficiency or uniformity of [SOURCE: Page_107]

distribution, as saving water is simply not a priority in their evaluative frame of [SOURCE: Page_107]

reference (Leeuwis and van den Ban, 2004). Their aspirations to become “drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_107]

farmers” may be agro-­‐economic (increasing yields, producing high quality products), [SOURCE: Page_107]

improving social status, or obtaining knowledge in order to play a role in the drip [SOURCE: Page_107]

irrigation support sector (councillors, fitters, salesmen). In fact, the combination of these [SOURCE: Page_107]

aspirations, which converge in individual farmers, may explain part of farmers’ [SOURCE: Page_107]

enthusiasm for drip irrigation (Zwarteveen et al. 2012). Irrigation performance, as [SOURCE: Page_107]

engineers see it, is at best only an intermediary objective for most farmers. Certain [SOURCE: Page_107]

farmers may be interested in achieving good distribution uniformity to ensure good [SOURCE: Page_107]

yields, but if they can compensate for poor uniformity by increasing the volume of water [SOURCE: Page_107]

they use for irrigation, with no adverse effects on yields, there may be no reason for [SOURCE: Page_107]

them to improve irrigation performance. [SOURCE: Page_107]

Secondly, irrigation performance should be seen more as a process than as a static, [SOURCE: Page_107]

unchanging value. This is related to the farmers’ belief that they are not only able to [SOURCE: Page_107]

change their practices when they adopt drip irrigation (Leeuwis and van den Ban 2004), [SOURCE: Page_107]

but also their ability to improve their (drip irrigation) practices as they go along. This [SOURCE: Page_107]

was the case of the farmers in the “learners” group, who introduced drip irrigation, were [SOURCE: Page_107]

not yet experts at all the practices, but were nevertheless confident they would learn [SOURCE: Page_107]

them in a very short time, thereby probably improving their irrigation performance. [SOURCE: Page_107]

From a more general point of view, the model proposed by Leeuwis and van den Ban [SOURCE: Page_107]

(2004) is explicitly presented as a dynamic system in “continuous motion”, through [SOURCE: Page_107]

which “social actors connect their past, present and future”. These dynamics are not only [SOURCE: Page_107]

the result of feed-­‐back mechanisms (learning, for instance), but are also related to [SOURCE: Page_107]

changes in the social environment (local expertise of drip irrigation equipment [SOURCE: Page_107]

manufacturers), or in changes in the aspirations of farmers or in their perceived self-­‐ efficacy. Farmers may thus change category from one year to the next as they become [SOURCE: Page_107]

more confident or less interested in agriculture; new categories may appear and others [SOURCE: Page_107]

disappear, and drip equipment may be improved or deteriorate. From a research point [SOURCE: Page_107]

of view, this means that irrigation performance should be monitored as a dynamic [SOURCE: Page_107]

process, efficiency and uniformity should be measured and farmers’ practices observed [SOURCE: Page_107]

over a longer period of time. [SOURCE: Page_107]

Thirdly, we showed how the wider social environment, which facilitated the [SOURCE: Page_107]

introduction and use of drip irrigation, was established progressively. Local expertise [SOURCE: Page_107]

developed that facilitated the purchase of equipment, the design and installation of the [SOURCE: Page_107]

system, its maintenance, and irrigation practices. This allowed equipment to be designed [SOURCE: Page_107]

for each particular situation. In addition, farmers had the support of strong social [SOURCE: Page_107]

networks in the use of drip irrigation. They regularly met and discussed different [SOURCE: Page_107]

practices related to drip irrigation with family members, neighbours, and friends. The [SOURCE: Page_107]

107 [SOURCE: Page_107]

presence of a drip irrigation “community” doubtless helped new farmers adopt drip [SOURCE: Page_108]

irrigation, but likely also influenced practices, and consequently irrigation performance. [SOURCE: Page_108]

For the time being, irrigation performance does not appear to be an important topic in [SOURCE: Page_108]

these networks, since, as mentioned above, farmers’ aspirations do not include [SOURCE: Page_108]

performing well from a hydraulic point of view. [SOURCE: Page_108]

Fourthly, there appears to be no social pressure on irrigating carefully to save water. [SOURCE: Page_108]

There is only one actor, the state, who explicitly links the use of drip irrigation with [SOURCE: Page_108]

saving water. However, the water saving objective is mixed with a water productivity [SOURCE: Page_108]

objective (more crop per drop), which makes the message rather confusing for farmers. [SOURCE: Page_108]

In addition, we almost never observed water meters on the drip systems, even on those [SOURCE: Page_108]

that had been subsidized by the state. In other words, the water saving message did not [SOURCE: Page_108]

get through to those who use drip irrigation systems, which are promoted to help save [SOURCE: Page_108]

water through greater efficiency and a uniformity of distribution. This leads generally to [SOURCE: Page_108]

over-­‐irrigation, and added pressure on water resources. These results are in accordance [SOURCE: Page_108]

with findings in India, where the adoption of drip irrigation led to increased pressure on [SOURCE: Page_108]

groundwater (Namara et al. 2007). [SOURCE: Page_108]

These four sets of variables put the concept of irrigation performance in a wider [SOURCE: Page_108]

perspective, thereby explaining the social nature of farmers’ practices, and the wide [SOURCE: Page_108]

range of hydraulic performance observed. Measuring hydraulic performance also helped [SOURCE: Page_108]

identify the logic underlying farmers’ practices. Frequently, the farmer’s stated logic of [SOURCE: Page_108]

irrigation corresponded to the logic of the “reference” farmers with whom they [SOURCE: Page_108]

identified. The difference in the irrigation performance of two farmers who claimed to [SOURCE: Page_108]

belong to the same category, prompted us to take a closer look at irrigation practices [SOURCE: Page_108]

and pinpoint a mismatch between farmers’ discourse and their actual practices. One of [SOURCE: Page_108]

the farmers, who considered he belonged to the “reference farmers” category, was seen [SOURCE: Page_108]

to possess all the capacities required to be in this group, but was not sufficiently present [SOURCE: Page_108]

in the field to direct irrigation practices and the maintenance of his equipment. This [SOURCE: Page_108]

farmer can be considered more as a councillor than as a practitioner of drip irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_108]

The field measurements also helped us to overcome our own previously biased view of [SOURCE: Page_108]

the performance of drip irrigation when we discovered that the large landholders’ [SOURCE: Page_108]

expensive high-­‐tech equipment had the lowest distribution uniformity. [SOURCE: Page_108]

### 5. Conclusion: Putting the user at the heart of the irrigation performance debate

Drip irrigation is a common topic in the international literature, and is often viewed as a [SOURCE: Page_108]

technical innovation leading to more efficient irrigation and water saving. Our results [SOURCE: Page_108]

show that the use of drip irrigation does not lead automatically to water saving, even at [SOURCE: Page_108]

the plot level. Farmers’ practices do not necessarily target “perfect” irrigation in the [SOURCE: Page_108]

sense hoped by engineers. Yet, the actors who install, use and maintain drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_108]

systems, and who consequently determine the actual irrigation performance of these [SOURCE: Page_108]

systems, remain invisible to researchers, and to the policy makers who subsidize drip [SOURCE: Page_108]

irrigation equipment. As a result, the efficient performance of drip irrigation is a myth. [SOURCE: Page_108]

108 [SOURCE: Page_108]

Encouraging the introduction of drip irrigation is often considered sufficient to deal with [SOURCE: Page_109]

problems of water scarcity. This means that little research is undertaken on how to [SOURCE: Page_109]

improve actual irrigation performance. Putting the user first in research studies would [SOURCE: Page_109]

probably lead to more modest assessments of actual irrigation performance, but could [SOURCE: Page_109]

also draw the attention of policy makers to the actual conditions in which drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_109]

is implemented, and thus to including ‘water saving’ as an objective for individual [SOURCE: Page_109]

farmers, and for the “drip irrigation community” as a whole. Our results also showed [SOURCE: Page_109]

that farmers were able to improve irrigation practices over a relatively short time, for [SOURCE: Page_109]

example to improve yields. This means that once water saving becomes part of their [SOURCE: Page_109]

objectives, farmers will certainly be able to increase the irrigation efficiency. [SOURCE: Page_109]

From a methodological point of view, our approach combined the measurement of [SOURCE: Page_109]

irrigation performance, the observation of irrigation practices, and the analysis of the [SOURCE: Page_109]

underlying logic of farmers who use drip irrigation. This approach enabled us to explain [SOURCE: Page_109]

the diversity and dynamics of irrigation performances in the field. While this may [SOURCE: Page_109]

initially appear to complicate the analysis of irrigation performance, we believe that [SOURCE: Page_109]

putting the actors concerned at the heart of the debate on irrigation performance (i.e. [SOURCE: Page_109]

making them visible) will make it possible to turn the myth of ‘saving water’ through [SOURCE: Page_109]

drip irrigation into reality. [SOURCE: Page_109]
