## 10.1080/1389224X.2014.977307

Aujourd’hui, les systèmes d'innovation sont considérés comme un concept utile pour [SOURCE: Page_112]

comprendre comment une multitude d’acteurs interagissent, échangent des [SOURCE: Page_112]

informations, des connaissances et développent, diffusent et utilisent les innovations [SOURCE: Page_112]

(Edquist 2005 ; Ortiz et al 2013). Akrich et al. (2002) ont proposé une théorie de [SOURCE: Page_112]

l'innovation en mettant l'accent sur l'étude de « l'innovation en train de se faire » en [SOURCE: Page_112]

reconstruisant les perspectives et les projets de tous les acteurs impliqués dans un [SOURCE: Page_112]

processus d'innovation. Ils se réfèrent à ces acteurs comme des « alliés ». L'innovation [SOURCE: Page_112]

est ainsi « l'art d'intéresser un nombre croissant d'alliés » où l’objet technique est intégré [SOURCE: Page_112]

dans un « réseau d’acteurs qui la reprennent, la soutiennent, la déplacent ». Dans ce [SOURCE: Page_112]

chapitre, nous postulons que ce modèle d’intéressement a rapidement remplacé le [SOURCE: Page_112]

modèle linéaire d’innovation tel qu’envisagé par les acteurs formels au début du [SOURCE: Page_112]

processus d’introduction du goute à goutte (grandes entreprises d’irrigation, [SOURCE: Page_112]

notamment). Le succès d'une innovation (sa diffusion et son adoption) dépend alors de [SOURCE: Page_112]

la participation active de tous ceux qui sont engagés à faire avancer la cause ; tout au [SOURCE: Page_112]

long de ce processus, la technologie est transformée. Ceci est illustré dans ce chapitre [SOURCE: Page_112]

par l'analyse des quatre moments de la traduction dans le processus d'innovation [SOURCE: Page_112]

(Callon 1986). [SOURCE: Page_112]

Dans notre étude, nous avons voulu en particulier comprendre les motivations qui [SOURCE: Page_112]

poussent des acteurs non-­‐conventionnels à intégrer le processus d’innovation. A travers [SOURCE: Page_112]

notre thèse, nous postulons que la diffusion rapide du goutte à goutte sur le terrain est [SOURCE: Page_112]

due essentiellement à ces acteurs non-­‐conventionnels qui ont pris en main l’innovation [SOURCE: Page_112]

et en même temps l’innovation les a pris en main. Ceci est dû aux multiples attributs du [SOURCE: Page_112]

goutte à goutte, qui ont pu séduire un nombre important d’acteurs non conventionnels. [SOURCE: Page_112]

Ces acteurs ont changé la technologie mais cette dernière a changé aussi leur parcours [SOURCE: Page_112]

socioprofessionnels. [SOURCE: Page_112]

Ce chapitre permet d’enrichir le débat scientifique récent sur les intermédiaires de [SOURCE: Page_112]

l’innovation (e.g. Howell, 2006 ; Klerkx et Leeuwis, 2008). Ces études mettent en avant [SOURCE: Page_112]

les rôles et fonctions de ces intermédiaires dans la circulation des information et [SOURCE: Page_112]

connaissances, et plus largement dans la production, utilisation et diffusion de [SOURCE: Page_112]

l’innovation (Geels, 2004). Cependant, peu d’études s’interrogent sur les motivations qui [SOURCE: Page_112]

à [SOURCE: Page_112]

112 [SOURCE: Page_112]

poussent ces acteurs à intégrer le processus de l’innovation. Notre étude aide à [SOURCE: Page_113]

comprendre les multiples voies des processus d'innovation, en interaction avec les [SOURCE: Page_113]

trajectoires socioprofessionnelles des acteurs non conventionnels de l'innovation. Nous [SOURCE: Page_113]

concluons qu’il est utile d'envisager l'intégration de ces acteurs dans les programmes de [SOURCE: Page_113]

l'État et d'autres processus d'innovation, tels que les grands programmes de [SOURCE: Page_113]

développement de l’irrigation, souvent impulsés par l'État. Souvent considérés comme [SOURCE: Page_113]

des non professionnels ou même des imposteurs, nous concluons que ces acteurs non-­‐ conventionnels devraient être considérés comme des alliés dans ces processus [SOURCE: Page_113]

d'innovation. Ils sont proches de la réalité du terrain et des utilisateurs de l'innovation. [SOURCE: Page_113]

Ils sont également capables d'adapter une technologie tout en tenant compte des [SOURCE: Page_113]

nécessités locales, ayant une parfaite connaissance du terrain. Ils ont fait leur preuve en [SOURCE: Page_113]

mettant en place un goutte à goutte « populaire », de proximité. Considérer ces acteurs [SOURCE: Page_113]

comme des alliés va certainement les motiver, car nous avons montré qu’ils sont [SOURCE: Page_113]

souvent intéressés par une reconnaissance institutionnelle. [SOURCE: Page_113]

Ce chapitre se présente comme suite : une première partie décrit l’enrôlement des [SOURCE: Page_113]

acteurs non-­‐conventionnels au cours de trois instances d’innovation. Dans une [SOURCE: Page_113]

deuxième section, les trajectoires de quatre acteurs non-­‐conventionnelles, impliqués [SOURCE: Page_113]

dans la production, l’utilisation et la diffusion de l'innovation sont analysées pour [SOURCE: Page_113]

expliquer les motivations de leur implication dans le processus d’innovation du goutte à [SOURCE: Page_113]

goutte, et décrire leur rôle dans ce processus. Et on termine par expliquer les [SOURCE: Page_113]

engagements des acteurs non conventionnels dans le processus de diffusion du goutte à [SOURCE: Page_113]

goutte. [SOURCE: Page_113]

irrigation community in Morocco [SOURCE: Page_114]

Purpose [SOURCE: Page_114]

The aim of this study was to analyze the motivations of non-­‐conventional innovation [SOURCE: Page_114]

actors to engage in innovation processes, how their involvement changed the technology [SOURCE: Page_114]

and their own social-­‐professional status, and to analyze their role in the diffusion of the [SOURCE: Page_114]

innovation. [SOURCE: Page_114]

Design/Methodology/Approach [SOURCE: Page_114]

We studied the innovation process of drip irrigation in Morocco. We interviewed 35 [SOURCE: Page_114]

farmers in two villages, selected to represent a diversity of farms, and observed their [SOURCE: Page_114]

drip irrigation systems. We interviewed several local artisans and traders, and [SOURCE: Page_114]

intermediaries about their social-­‐professional pathway, using a checklist to understand [SOURCE: Page_114]

their motivations and their involvement with drip irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_114]

Findings [SOURCE: Page_114]

We showed how a variety of non-­‐conventional actors became involved in drip irrigation, [SOURCE: Page_114]

leading to the progressive creation of an active inter-­‐related socio-­‐technical network [SOURCE: Page_114]

involved in the sales, manufacturing, fitting, and use of drip irrigation systems. This [SOURCE: Page_114]

network challenged an imported technology promoted by irrigation companies that [SOURCE: Page_114]

targeted large-­‐scale farmers, and transformed it into drip irrigation systems adapted to [SOURCE: Page_114]

a wide range of situations and farmers, including small-­‐scale farmers. The involvement [SOURCE: Page_114]

of these actors led to reciprocal changes in the technology and in the socio-­‐professional [SOURCE: Page_114]

status of the intermediaries, hence accelerating the diffusion of the innovation. [SOURCE: Page_114]

114 [SOURCE: Page_114]

Practical implications [SOURCE: Page_115]

Understanding the motivations of non-­‐conventional innovation actors helps [SOURCE: Page_115]

comprehend the multiple pathways of innovation processes, and the socio-­‐professional [SOURCE: Page_115]

pathways of innovation actors. It is worth considering integrating these actors in state [SOURCE: Page_115]

programs and other planned innovation processes, as they are near to field realities and [SOURCE: Page_115]

to innovation users, and are able to adapt a technology to local requirements. [SOURCE: Page_115]

Originality/Value [SOURCE: Page_115]

The results of this study contribute to the scientific debate about the mutually beneficial [SOURCE: Page_115]

alliance of non-­‐conventional actors and technical innovations. [SOURCE: Page_115]

Key words [SOURCE: Page_115]

Innovation, intermediaries, enrolment, diffusion, drip irrigation, Morocco [SOURCE: Page_115]

Paper type [SOURCE: Page_115]

Research paper [SOURCE: Page_115]

### 1. Introduction

Innovation systems research has changed the analytical perspective of innovation into a [SOURCE: Page_115]

more complex, process-­‐based systems approach (Spielman, Ekboir, and Davis 2009). [SOURCE: Page_115]

Innovation systems have been shown to be a useful concept for understanding how [SOURCE: Page_115]

multiple actors interact, exchange information, generate knowledge and develop, [SOURCE: Page_115]

diffuse, and use innovations (Edquist 2005; Ortiz et al. 2013). Akrich, Callon, and Latour [SOURCE: Page_115]

(2002) proposed an innovation theory focusing on the study of ‘innovation in the [SOURCE: Page_115]

making’ by reconstructing the perspectives and projects of all actors involved in an [SOURCE: Page_115]

innovation process. They referred to these actors as ‘allies’, as innovation is “the art of [SOURCE: Page_115]

interesting an increasing number of allies”, as the innovation integrates itself “into a [SOURCE: Page_115]

network of actors who take it up, support it, diffuse it”. This model of ‘interessement’ [SOURCE: Page_115]

explaining the success of an innovation thus draws attention to “the points of articulation [SOURCE: Page_115]

between the object and the more or less organised interests which it gives rise to” (ibid). [SOURCE: Page_115]

Socio-­‐technical networks are thus composite systems, combining humans and non-­‐ humans (actants) and even concepts, and also hybrid systems as they pertain both to the [SOURCE: Page_115]

design, the use, and the diffusion of the innovation (Callon, 2004). [SOURCE: Page_115]

Mapping the “effective multitude” of actors and actants, and their interactions, in an [SOURCE: Page_115]

innovation in the making (Akrich, Callon, and Latour 2002) constitutes a methodological [SOURCE: Page_115]

challenge, as field realities are messy, and innovation actors often difficult to identify. In [SOURCE: Page_115]

115 [SOURCE: Page_115]

agriculture, many authors have shown that conventional actors, such as the agricultural [SOURCE: Page_116]

extension services, are not the only innovation actors. Innovation actors may be local [SOURCE: Page_116]

farmers, innovation intermediaries and rural artisans or traders involved in ‘second [SOURCE: Page_116]

order innovations’, who “rewrite the grammar of innovation, change the relevant [SOURCE: Page_116]

knowledge and even the relevant objectives of innovations” (Knickel et al. 2009). Poncet, [SOURCE: Page_116]

Kuper, and Chiche (2010) illustrated how in Morocco many local innovations were the [SOURCE: Page_116]

result of farmers’ initiatives, and involved a wide range of non-­‐conventional actors, i.e. [SOURCE: Page_116]

actors for whom the involvement in the innovation process may be an informal or a side [SOURCE: Page_116]

activity, or even fellow farmers. Howells (2006) developed a framework to analyze the [SOURCE: Page_116]

roles and functions of what he called innovation ‘intermediaries’, who act as agents or [SOURCE: Page_116]

brokers in innovation processes. Such intermediary functions include demand [SOURCE: Page_116]

articulation, network formation, and innovation process management (Klerkx and [SOURCE: Page_116]

Leeuwis 2009; Kilelu, Klerkx and Leeuwis 2013). Friederichsen et al. (2013) indicated [SOURCE: Page_116]

that intermediaries are a central actor in innovation systems due to their function to [SOURCE: Page_116]

bridge informational gaps and mediate between the interests of actors comprising the [SOURCE: Page_116]

system. [SOURCE: Page_116]

In this paper, we suggest that non-­‐conventional innovation actors join the innovation [SOURCE: Page_116]

process not with the aim of diffusion, but rather because of social-­‐professional [SOURCE: Page_116]

motivations. The aim of this study was to analyze the motivations of innovation actors [SOURCE: Page_116]

who engaged in an innovation process, how their involvement changed the technology [SOURCE: Page_116]

and their own social-­‐professional status, and to analyze their role in the diffusion of the [SOURCE: Page_116]

innovation. In doing so, we aim to contribute to opening the “black box of innovation to [SOURCE: Page_116]

analyze actors’ motives and behaviors” (Spielman 2005). Following Laschewski, [SOURCE: Page_116]

Phillipson, and Gorton (2002), our underlying hypothesis is that such actors cannot be [SOURCE: Page_116]

neutral or impartial in innovation processes as they co-­‐steer these processes, and the [SOURCE: Page_116]

functioning of innovation networks depends on their personal engagement (Klerkx and [SOURCE: Page_116]

Leeuwis 2009). We also hypothesized that social capital was central in the socio-­‐ professional pathways of the innovation actors in relation to the innovation process. [SOURCE: Page_116]

Following Bourdieu (1980), we defined social capital as the set of resources, which are [SOURCE: Page_116]

linked to possessing a network of more or less institutionalized relations, based on [SOURCE: Page_116]

mutual cognition and recognition. In our case, the expertise of the innovation actors is [SOURCE: Page_116]

known and recognized by themselves, by others (e.g. the farmers or even the state [SOURCE: Page_116]

services) or even by external observers. [SOURCE: Page_116]

This study concerned the diffusion of drip irrigation in Morocco. Drip irrigation is a [SOURCE: Page_116]

pressurized irrigation technology that supplies water directly to the vicinity of the [SOURCE: Page_116]

plants through perforated plastic tubes. It is advocated for its water use efficiency, as [SOURCE: Page_116]

well as for improving yields while reducing labor requirements (Cornish 1998). This [SOURCE: Page_116]

was a particularly interesting case study for us, as drip irrigation was originally thought [SOURCE: Page_116]

to be only suitable for high-­‐tech large-­‐scale farms, but recent studies showed that it [SOURCE: Page_116]

could be adapted by local actors, making it accessible to a wide range of users, including [SOURCE: Page_116]

small-­‐scale farmers (Ameur et al. 2013; Benouniche, Zwarteveen, and Kuper 2014). The [SOURCE: Page_116]

116 [SOURCE: Page_116]

results of this study should contribute to the scientific debate about the mutually [SOURCE: Page_117]

beneficial alliance of non-­‐conventional actors and technical innovations. [SOURCE: Page_117]

### 2. Methodology

The study area is located in the Saiss, a rich agricultural area south of the cities of [SOURCE: Page_117]

Meknes and Fes in northwest Morocco. From the 1980s onwards, farming systems in the [SOURCE: Page_117]

Saiss were transformed from mainly rain-­‐fed agriculture (cereals, vineyards) to [SOURCE: Page_117]

irrigated agriculture as farmers progressively turned to groundwater use. This led to a [SOURCE: Page_117]

significant increase in the area under fruit trees (42% of the irrigated area), and [SOURCE: Page_117]

horticulture (35%). The Ministry of Agriculture estimated the current irrigated area in [SOURCE: Page_117]

2012 to be 49,677 ha, of which 45,316 ha depended on pump irrigation through more [SOURCE: Page_117]

than 12,000 individual (tube) wells. Half of the irrigated area is irrigated by drip [SOURCE: Page_117]

irrigation. The study concerned two villages, which reflect the dynamics of two different [SOURCE: Page_117]

realities regarding the diffusion of drip irrigation. The differences between the two [SOURCE: Page_117]

villages related to the timing of the introduction of drip irrigation, and to the types of [SOURCE: Page_117]

farmers implementing drip irrigation. In the first village, drip irrigation appeared with [SOURCE: Page_117]

the arrival of large investors in the early 1990s and was adopted by medium and small-­‐ scale farmers from 2000 onwards (Ameur et al, 2013). In the second village, the [SOURCE: Page_117]

development of drip irrigation occurred later. Most large (absentee) farmers in this [SOURCE: Page_117]

village did not practice irrigated agriculture, and it was mostly the small holders who [SOURCE: Page_117]

engaged in drip irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_117]

Following the objectives of this paper, we first identified the non-­‐conventional [SOURCE: Page_117]

innovation actors, i.e. local artisans, traders, innovation intermediaries or even fellow [SOURCE: Page_117]

farmers, involved in diffusion of drip irrigation. This is very complex, as most actors are [SOURCE: Page_117]

not officially registered, and their involvement in drip irrigation continuously changes. [SOURCE: Page_117]

In addition, a particular non-­‐conventional innovation actor may be involved in different [SOURCE: Page_117]

activities. We thus identified these actors through interviews with farmers adopting drip [SOURCE: Page_117]

irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_117]

We hypothesized that specific innovation actors would not necessarily have interacted [SOURCE: Page_117]

with all types of farmers, as 1) their drip irrigation systems may be different, 2) they [SOURCE: Page_117]

introduced drip irrigation during different periods, 3) in case of state subsidies, certain [SOURCE: Page_117]

informal actors may not be involved. In order to identify all non-­‐conventional actors that [SOURCE: Page_117]

were active in the study area, we aimed to select and then interview the largest possible [SOURCE: Page_117]

number of farmers who had installed drip irrigation in the past 25 years in the two [SOURCE: Page_117]

villages. We interviewed a total of 35 farmers (out of 44 farmers who had adopted drip [SOURCE: Page_117]

irrigation), representing a diversity of farm types: 1) Large-­‐scale farmers (6 in our [SOURCE: Page_117]

sample) who first introduced drip irrigation in their large-­‐scale farms (>100 ha); they [SOURCE: Page_117]

practice four different farming systems: orchards, horticulture, livestock, and cereals, 2) [SOURCE: Page_117]

medium-­‐scale (10-­‐15 ha) horticultural farmers (5) specialize in the production of onions [SOURCE: Page_117]

and potatoes, 3) small-­‐scale horticultural farmers (24) represent the majority of small-­‐ scale farmers (<5 ha); they specialize in the production of onions and potatoes. We [SOURCE: Page_117]

117 [SOURCE: Page_117]

asked the selected farmers about how they fitted drip irrigation (who they contacted, [SOURCE: Page_118]

when, and how they installed the system), who sold them the equipment, and who [SOURCE: Page_118]

advised them during this process. [SOURCE: Page_118]

We thus identified, and then interviewed 13 non-­‐conventional actors named by the [SOURCE: Page_118]

farmers for their role in the innovation process about their activities and functions, [SOURCE: Page_118]

using a checklist. We distinguished the different types of intermediaries, artisans and [SOURCE: Page_118]

traders involved: 1) fitters help farmers to install the drip irrigation system in the field, [SOURCE: Page_118]

2) welders make repairs to the equipment (pumps, engines, filters etc.); certain welders [SOURCE: Page_118]

even fabricate certain components of the drip irrigation system, thus becoming [SOURCE: Page_118]

recognized manufacturers, 3) retailers sell irrigation equipment to farmers, and 4) [SOURCE: Page_118]

advisors make the link between farmers and different artisans and traders. This enabled [SOURCE: Page_118]

mapping the involvement of non-­‐conventional actors in the diffusion of drip irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_118]

We also interviewed engineers of the Ministry of Agriculture, in charge of subsidy [SOURCE: Page_118]

procedures, and engineers of private companies, to cross-­‐check the information [SOURCE: Page_118]

provided by farmers and intermediaries. [SOURCE: Page_118]

The names of four actors came up repeatedly in the interviews, as persons known and [SOURCE: Page_118]

recognized for their expertise in delivering an adapted drip irrigation system to a wide [SOURCE: Page_118]

range of farmers in the study area. These four actors had handled the implementation of [SOURCE: Page_118]

the vast majority of drip irrigation systems in the study area. We decided to undertake [SOURCE: Page_118]

detailed interviews with these actors, i.e. 1) a small-­‐scale farmer who had adapted drip [SOURCE: Page_118]

irrigation systems to small-­‐scale farmers and later became a fitter, 2) a fitter for small-­‐ scale farmers, who had obtained his knowledge in large-­‐scale farms, 3) a manufacturer [SOURCE: Page_118]

of local equipment, who was also strongly involved in the repairs of drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_118]

systems, and 4) a retailer selling imported and local equipment. We interviewed them [SOURCE: Page_118]

about their social-­‐professional pathway, using a checklist (when and how they learned [SOURCE: Page_118]

to use drip irrigation, their knowledge, their functions, their network, their professional [SOURCE: Page_118]

activities) to understand their motivation and how they became involved with drip [SOURCE: Page_118]

irrigation. We observed how they worked and with whom, and how they interacted with [SOURCE: Page_118]

other actors. We also interviewed their customers, i.e. the sample farmers, on the choice [SOURCE: Page_118]

of these intermediaries. [SOURCE: Page_118]

### 3. Results

3.1 The enrolment of non-­‐conventional actors in three innovation periods [SOURCE: Page_118]

The diffusion of drip irrigation in the study area was rapid. We identified three [SOURCE: Page_118]

innovation periods (fig. 1) (Ameur et al. 2013). In the first period, large-­‐scale farmers [SOURCE: Page_118]

progressively introduced drip irrigation, mainly for fruit trees. The large farms in the [SOURCE: Page_118]

second village (often absentee farmers) did not practice irrigated agriculture, which [SOURCE: Page_118]

delayed its diffusion there. During the second period, little extension occurred in either [SOURCE: Page_118]

village, but medium and small-­‐scale farmers experimented with drip irrigation for [SOURCE: Page_118]

horticultural crops. In the third period, a large number of small-­‐scale horticultural [SOURCE: Page_118]

farmers adopted drip irrigation leading to its rapid diffusion. In 2012, 224 ha (44% of [SOURCE: Page_118]

118 [SOURCE: Page_118]

the agricultural area) were under drip irrigation in the first village, and 178 ha (27%) in [SOURCE: Page_119]

the second, concerning almost 30% of the farmers in both villages. [SOURCE: Page_119]

Figure 1 Increase in the area equipped with drip irrigation in two villages in three [SOURCE: Page_119]

successive innovation periods. [SOURCE: Page_119]

The three innovation periods are linked to the development of three socio-­‐technical [SOURCE: Page_119]

networks involving non-­‐conventional actors (fig. 2). The figure shows the multitude of [SOURCE: Page_119]

actors and actants involved in the process: 1) large-­‐scale and small-­‐scale farmers, and [SOURCE: Page_119]

their farm managers and laborers, 2) local artisans and traders, including retailers, [SOURCE: Page_119]

welders, and fitters, 3) intermediaries making the link between different actors, and [SOURCE: Page_119]

actants, and providing information and knowledge (farmers, local artisans and traders, [SOURCE: Page_119]

advisors), 4) engineers of private irrigation companies or of the agricultural services in [SOURCE: Page_119]

charge of the subsidy procedure, and 5) different drip irrigation systems (DIS), and the [SOURCE: Page_119]

components of these systems. In the three innovation periods these actors successively [SOURCE: Page_119]

1) got involved, 2) re-­‐engineered drip irrigation systems adapting them to local [SOURCE: Page_119]

conditions, and 3) became important (but fewer) innovation actors who provided [SOURCE: Page_119]

different clients with complete projects. [SOURCE: Page_119]

Learning by doing: involvement of non-­‐conventional actors (1990-­‐2000) [SOURCE: Page_119]

In the first innovation period (1990–2000), irrigation companies from Casablanca -­‐the [SOURCE: Page_119]

major entry point into Morocco for imported merchandise-­‐ proposed imported [SOURCE: Page_119]

equipment to large-­‐scale farmers. These farmers progressively introduced drip [SOURCE: Page_119]

irrigation, mainly for fruit trees. This showed that drip irrigation could work in local [SOURCE: Page_119]

119 [SOURCE: Page_119]

conditions (soils, farming systems). This first socio-­‐technical network was important for [SOURCE: Page_120]

future diffusion, as non-­‐conventional actors became involved in the drip irrigation world [SOURCE: Page_120]

(fig. 2). Conventional actors (irrigation companies, linked to (inter)national technology [SOURCE: Page_120]

providers) were based more than 200 km away, and business was insufficient for [SOURCE: Page_120]

effective local after-­‐sales service. Large-­‐scale farm owners were rarely involved in day-­‐ to-­‐day operations, because they have another professional status in nearby towns [SOURCE: Page_120]

(liberal professions) or they are office bearers in farmers’ organizations, e.g. the regional [SOURCE: Page_120]

dairy cooperative, so they are often absent. Farm managers thus supervised the [SOURCE: Page_120]

installation and use of drip irrigation, and farm laborers actually installed it, alongside [SOURCE: Page_120]

company technicians. Managers and laborers directly handled the equipment, and when [SOURCE: Page_120]

necessary repaired it. Without much preparation, learning on the job, they gradually [SOURCE: Page_120]

acquired experience in the fitting, and operation and maintenance (O&M) of drip [SOURCE: Page_120]

irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_120]

During this period, these farm managers and laborers prepared for the functions they [SOURCE: Page_120]

would later play in the innovation process as advisors, fitters or retailers. Firstly, farm [SOURCE: Page_120]

managers and laborers progressively created a demand, both for the technology itself [SOURCE: Page_120]

and for the supply and repair of spare parts. Secondly, multi-­‐actor drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_120]

networks were progressively installed. Farm managers and specialized (irrigation) [SOURCE: Page_120]

laborers developed working relations with company technicians, local retailers and [SOURCE: Page_120]

welders, while supervising the running of the drip irrigation systems. Thirdly, by making [SOURCE: Page_120]

these networks work, farm managers and laborers facilitated the alignment of these [SOURCE: Page_120]

networks through functional links and enabled further learning on making drip [SOURCE: Page_120]

irrigation work. Different actors got used to working together while dealing with the [SOURCE: Page_120]

daily operational problems of drip irrigation; they were recruited in the drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_120]

world while making imported equipment work. [SOURCE: Page_120]

120 [SOURCE: Page_120]

Figure 2 Involvement of non-­‐conventional actors in the diffusion of drip irrigation in [SOURCE: Page_121]

three different socio-­‐technical systems. [SOURCE: Page_121]

Non-­‐conventional actors developed their own niche by creating new trades (2000-­‐2005) [SOURCE: Page_121]

In this second period (2000-­‐2005), large-­‐scale farmers extended drip irrigation to other [SOURCE: Page_121]

plots. Their laborers now installed the equipment by themselves, which provided more [SOURCE: Page_121]

learning opportunities. This also increased the area under drip irrigation, and created [SOURCE: Page_121]

more demand for drip equipment, and repair services. Large-­‐scale farmers became the [SOURCE: Page_121]

drivers of an emerging local support sector. A second socio-­‐technical network emerged [SOURCE: Page_121]

that targeted the development, diffusion and use of drip irrigation for horticulture on [SOURCE: Page_121]

small-­‐scale farms. It built on the knowledge and experience acquired in the first [SOURCE: Page_121]

network. Some laborers installed drip irrigation on their own small farms, mostly for [SOURCE: Page_121]

horticultural crops (potatoes, onions). These non-­‐conventional actors adapted the [SOURCE: Page_121]

technology, developing simpler and less costly drip irrigation systems. Laborers became [SOURCE: Page_121]

drip farmers, fitters, and advisors. Local fitters or welders became retailers or [SOURCE: Page_121]

manufacturers of drip irrigation equipment. The local support sector catered for both [SOURCE: Page_121]

networks, although some actors specialized in systems for large or small-­‐scale farms. [SOURCE: Page_121]

This was the start of a period of glory for such actors; their social-­‐professional status [SOURCE: Page_121]

improved as they created new trades. [SOURCE: Page_121]

121 [SOURCE: Page_121]

This innovation period was a time of rapid adaptation characterized by a re-­‐engineering [SOURCE: Page_122]

of drip irrigation systems by an emerging support sector made up of different non-­‐ conventional actors. A wide range of equipment was now available at local retailers, and [SOURCE: Page_122]

welders repaired and manufactured drip irrigation systems. Local fitters installed drip [SOURCE: Page_122]

irrigation systems on small-­‐scale farms, but in reality they fulfilled the different [SOURCE: Page_122]

functions generally associated with innovation intermediation. They adapted standard [SOURCE: Page_122]

technological packages to meet the specific requirements of farmers. This included low-­‐ cost equipment for farmers who wanted to experiment with drip irrigation, thereby [SOURCE: Page_122]

lowering the entry cost into the drip community. They also developed innovation [SOURCE: Page_122]

networks. There was at least one local fitter in each village, linking farmers and local [SOURCE: Page_122]

artisans and traders (retailers, manufacturers) (fig. 2). During this process, the farmers [SOURCE: Page_122]

generally discussed with the fitter about the technical choices (type of drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_122]

system, choice of equipment, design of the system), and about the choice of the artisans [SOURCE: Page_122]

and traders. The network was multi-­‐facetted, and farmers would try different [SOURCE: Page_122]

intermediaries to avoid monopoly situations. This maintained lively, competitive [SOURCE: Page_122]

networks in which intermediaries had to remain posted on new developments, deliver [SOURCE: Page_122]

appropriate services, and more generally respond to the needs of farmers. [SOURCE: Page_122]

Professionalization of the informal sector (2005–2012), involving conventional actors [SOURCE: Page_122]

In the third innovation period, drip irrigation spread fast, and was installed on all farm [SOURCE: Page_122]

types, including small-­‐scale horticultural farms. More and more farmers were interested [SOURCE: Page_122]

in drip irrigation, which had shown multiple advantages to farmers in the area [SOURCE: Page_122]

(increased yields, ease of use, social status etc.). Farmers had also better knowledge on [SOURCE: Page_122]

how to make drip irrigation work, and were supported by increasingly solid networks of [SOURCE: Page_122]

support services. This period was characterized by the progressive amalgamation of the [SOURCE: Page_122]

two existing socio-­‐technical networks, with the diffusion of drip irrigation to all types of [SOURCE: Page_122]

farmers. This amalgamation was facilitated by the development of a hybrid drip [SOURCE: Page_122]

irrigation system combining imported and locally manufactured components, which [SOURCE: Page_122]

progressively became the local standard. State subsidies to promote water saving and [SOURCE: Page_122]

water productivity (80-­‐100% of the investment cost of drip irrigation) encouraged the [SOURCE: Page_122]

new drip irrigation artisans and traders to formalize their activities, or to associate with [SOURCE: Page_122]

formal actors, such as the national irrigation companies. The formal actors were very [SOURCE: Page_122]

interested in extending their activities to the booming informal sector, and contacted [SOURCE: Page_122]

local fitters or retailers in the hope of delivering their services through them. Local [SOURCE: Page_122]

artisans and traders increased the scale of their operations, found new clients, and [SOURCE: Page_122]

professionalized their business, by catering to a diversity of often-­‐complex projects. As [SOURCE: Page_122]

competition increased, some drip irrigation intermediaries quit their jobs as fitters, and [SOURCE: Page_122]

farmers bypassed them by dealing directly with retailers or manufacturers. Other [SOURCE: Page_122]

intermediaries were able to continue their (informal) activities by sticking to classical [SOURCE: Page_122]

intermediary functions, for example by acting as an intermediary between drip [SOURCE: Page_122]

irrigation artisans and traders, and farmers. Such intermediaries played a pro-­‐active [SOURCE: Page_122]

role in finding new clients, and accompanied small-­‐scale farmers in their negotiations [SOURCE: Page_122]

with drip irrigation artisans and traders, for example to obtain credit facilities. [SOURCE: Page_122]

122 [SOURCE: Page_122]

3.2 The pathways of four non-­‐conventional innovation actors [SOURCE: Page_123]

In this section, the pathways of four non-­‐conventional innovation actors are analyzed to [SOURCE: Page_123]

explain their reasons for getting involved in drip irrigation, and describe their role in the [SOURCE: Page_123]

diffusion of the innovation (table 1). We take a closer look at their socio-­‐professional [SOURCE: Page_123]

motivations for engaging with drip irrigation and the attributes of the technology that [SOURCE: Page_123]

attracted them to drip irrigation (table 1). The pathways of two drip irrigation fitters, [SOURCE: Page_123]

who were intermediaries in the innovation process, are first described. The pathways of [SOURCE: Page_123]

a drip irrigation artisan, and of a trader are then presented. [SOURCE: Page_123]

Drip irrigation fitters [SOURCE: Page_123]

1) Driss: a jack of all trades [SOURCE: Page_123]

Driss was a specialized laborer on a big farm, in charge of the irrigation crew. In 2002, [SOURCE: Page_123]

he helped technicians from an irrigation company install a drip irrigation system. After [SOURCE: Page_123]

they left, Driss had to deal with daily operating problems, including clogging of emitters, [SOURCE: Page_123]

and repair of tubing (table 1). Next, his boss asked him to install a drip irrigation system [SOURCE: Page_123]

on a small estate close to the capital. This miniature system was based on the use of [SOURCE: Page_123]

small valves. The valves were cheap, making it possible to install drip irrigation on small [SOURCE: Page_123]

plots. This was an eye-­‐opener for Driss, who discovered that drip irrigation was not as [SOURCE: Page_123]

complicated or expensive as he had thought, and that it was possible to use it on a small [SOURCE: Page_123]

plot: "I started to imagine my own drip irrigation system" (Driss, 32 years, 2013). [SOURCE: Page_123]

Encouraged by the experience gained on the large-­‐scale farm, and the good relationship [SOURCE: Page_123]

with a local drip irrigation retailer, Driss installed drip irrigation on his own small-­‐scale [SOURCE: Page_123]

farm (< 1 ha). He became well known in his village: he was a small farmer, yet he had a [SOURCE: Page_123]

drip irrigation system like big and modern farmers, and he was even capable of fitting [SOURCE: Page_123]

such systems: "when I installed my drip irrigation system, everybody came to see it, my [SOURCE: Page_123]

father, my mother, and my neighbors, it was like a wedding party." (Driss, 32 years, 2013). [SOURCE: Page_123]

A few days later, a neighbor asked him to install a similar drip irrigation system. Driss [SOURCE: Page_123]

was able to test his know-­‐how on his own farm and on his neighbor’s, which increased [SOURCE: Page_123]

his self-­‐confidence. He found his niche as a fitter of drip irrigation for small farmers, and [SOURCE: Page_123]

as an intermediary between farmers and tradesmen (retailers, manufacturers). Driss [SOURCE: Page_123]

became “Mister” Driss; he left his job as a laborer on the big farm: "I felt so proud of [SOURCE: Page_123]

myself the day my neighbor asked me to install drip irrigation. He contacted me as a drip [SOURCE: Page_123]

irrigation specialist, not as a laborer" (Driss, 32 years, 2013). Driss became increasingly [SOURCE: Page_123]

involved in the emerging drip irrigation networks; his social status changed. [SOURCE: Page_123]

Today, Driss no longer installs drip irrigation systems because farmers can install it [SOURCE: Page_123]

themselves. However, he continued to be involved in the diffusion of drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_123]

because the modernity associated with this technique is important for his social status [SOURCE: Page_123]

(table 1). Farmers asked him for advice, and he helped them design their system, and [SOURCE: Page_123]

123 [SOURCE: Page_123]

then put them in touch with a retailer he knows, often free of charge. He remained active [SOURCE: Page_124]

in drip irrigation networks, both with the actors of the supply sector, and with laborers [SOURCE: Page_124]

on the large-­‐scale farms, so he was informed about new equipment, and new (drip [SOURCE: Page_124]

irrigation) practices. He travelled a lot in Morocco, and went to different international [SOURCE: Page_124]

and national shows and trade fairs. In addition, he remained involved in the small-­‐scale [SOURCE: Page_124]

family farm. [SOURCE: Page_124]

2) Aissam: drip irrigation as long as it goes [SOURCE: Page_124]

Aissam discovered drip irrigation when he was a farm manager on a large-­‐scale farm. In [SOURCE: Page_124]

1992, he helped fit 30 ha of orchards with drip irrigation, and supervised laborers in its [SOURCE: Page_124]

operation and maintenance (table 1). In 1996, Aissam quit his job when he got married, [SOURCE: Page_124]

and returned to his village where he farmed land belonging to a cousin. In 1998, he [SOURCE: Page_124]

accepted a job as a fitter with a local retailer: "My uncle suggested I work for Mohamed [SOURCE: Page_124]

(retailer); he needed laborers who knew how to fit drip irrigation. He had a lot of demand [SOURCE: Page_124]

from farmers; he was looking for laborers who had already installed drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_124]

systems on big farms” (Aissam, 43 years, 2013). He helped fit 5 ha of drip irrigation for [SOURCE: Page_124]

horticulture, and discovered it could be adapted to small-­‐scale horticulture. [SOURCE: Page_124]

Fifteen days later, Aissam’s uncle asked him to install drip irrigation on a 7-­‐ha plot in his [SOURCE: Page_124]

village. His uncle was very satisfied with the drip irrigation system and showed it to all [SOURCE: Page_124]

the villagers. This success gave Aissam self-­‐confidence and a certain reputation. [SOURCE: Page_124]

Attracted by the rapid diffusion of drip irrigation, he left his job as a farmer, and became [SOURCE: Page_124]

a full-­‐time fitter of drip irrigation for small-­‐scale farmers, making a living out of [SOURCE: Page_124]

installing drip irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_124]

However, the situation gradually changed: "2004 was the peak for drip irrigation, people [SOURCE: Page_124]

called on me in my own village and elsewhere. I had a lot of work and was paid very well. [SOURCE: Page_124]

But from 2010 on, demand fell. Today everyone knows how to install drip; nobody needs a [SOURCE: Page_124]

fitter. Drip irrigation does not bring me anything, there is no future in it, we can’t make a [SOURCE: Page_124]

living from it any more" (Aissam, 43 years, 2013). As a landless farmer he did not have a [SOURCE: Page_124]

“laboratory” to test and innovate with drip irrigation. He simply reproduced the same [SOURCE: Page_124]

drip irrigation system, which he installed for all the small-­‐scale horticultural farmers in [SOURCE: Page_124]

his village. With time, the technology of drip irrigation was demystified, and farmers no [SOURCE: Page_124]

longer required his services. He did not improve or expand his business, and other more [SOURCE: Page_124]

resourceful intermediaries took over. In 2011, Aissam left the drip irrigation sector and [SOURCE: Page_124]

started to work again as a farm laborer. Today, he works as a laborer at the dairy [SOURCE: Page_124]

cooperative, where his uncle -­‐ president of the cooperative -­‐ hired him. Unlike Driss, [SOURCE: Page_124]

when his income went down, he was in no position to continue in the drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_124]

world. In other words, drip irrigation was just synonym of making a living (table 1). [SOURCE: Page_124]

124 [SOURCE: Page_124]

Table 1 Professional activities, functions, motivations of four innovation actors, in [SOURCE: Page_125]

relation to the attributes of drip irrigation systems. [SOURCE: Page_125]

Drip irrigation artisans and traders [SOURCE: Page_125]

3) Mohamed: a welder and a pioneer manufacturer of irrigation equipment [SOURCE: Page_125]

Mohamed was a qualified technician. He was a trainee welder in a local workshop, and [SOURCE: Page_125]

after two years set up his own welding workshop. He repaired pumps, engines, and [SOURCE: Page_125]

plows (table 1). In 1998, a big farmer ordered a sand filter from him, because he thought [SOURCE: Page_125]

the imported filters were not strong enough. Mohamed bought an imported filter from a [SOURCE: Page_125]

nearby retailer, took it apart and copied it, using good quality metal sheet. The filter [SOURCE: Page_125]

turned out to be a success, thus providing excellent publicity for his work. In 2000, he [SOURCE: Page_125]

produced 30-­‐40 filters a year for farmers located as far as 30 km away. However, he [SOURCE: Page_125]

continued to look for other work, and was also known for transforming diesel into gas [SOURCE: Page_125]

engines, the demand for which increased rapidly from 2004 onwards. The trust farmers [SOURCE: Page_125]

had in his workmanship, and the extensive networks (farmers, retailers, suppliers) he [SOURCE: Page_125]

developed were based on drip irrigation, but Mohamed ensured he would not depend on [SOURCE: Page_125]

the (temporary) boom in the drip irrigation sector. [SOURCE: Page_125]

125 [SOURCE: Page_125]

From 2008 onwards, state subsidies increased to 80-­‐100% of the investment cost of [SOURCE: Page_126]

drip irrigation systems to promote water saving and water productivity. Farmers were [SOURCE: Page_126]

more demanding, as the products Mohamed manufactured had to qualify for the subsidy [SOURCE: Page_126]

procedure. Mohamed also had an increasing number of drip irrigation intermediaries [SOURCE: Page_126]

(retailers, fitters) as customers, who wanted to sell key-­‐in-­‐hand drip irrigation projects [SOURCE: Page_126]

to farmers. In 2010, he opened a shop to sell his products in the city of Meknes, and will [SOURCE: Page_126]

open another in 2013. As a trained mechanic, Mohamed was initially attracted by the [SOURCE: Page_126]

technicality of drip irrigation, but is increasingly looking for recognition as an able [SOURCE: Page_126]

manufacturer (table 1). He aims to show his products at the international agricultural [SOURCE: Page_126]

fair in Meknes, where he will be competing with (inter)national manufactures. [SOURCE: Page_126]

4) Younes: a retailer catering to different markets and a contractor for complex projects [SOURCE: Page_126]

Younes learned about drip irrigation in 1982 when he was a farm laborer and installed [SOURCE: Page_126]

drip irrigation (table 1). After 12 years as a specialized laborer in charge of the irrigation [SOURCE: Page_126]

crew, an opportunity arose in 1994: “At the time, very few people could handle drip [SOURCE: Page_126]

irrigation and with all the knowledge I had, I could not remain a laborer. I often went to [SOURCE: Page_126]

nearby towns on business. One day, the director of a consultancy firm in Meknes suggested [SOURCE: Page_126]

I join his team of fitters. I immediately accepted, and left my job as a laborer”. [SOURCE: Page_126]

Younes worked as a fitter with different consultancy firms, which allowed him to [SOURCE: Page_126]

develop extensive networks with farmers, intermediaries, and drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_126]

companies. His principal function was fitting, but he was involved in complex projects [SOURCE: Page_126]

(table 1). In 2005, a representative of one of the leading Casablanca drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_126]

companies encouraged him to set up as a retailer. The company found him the premises [SOURCE: Page_126]

in a nearby town, and supplied him with irrigation equipment. The company thus [SOURCE: Page_126]

obtained access to the emerging market of small-­‐scale horticultural farmers, while [SOURCE: Page_126]

Younes started a professional career as a retailer and a local representative of the drip [SOURCE: Page_126]

irrigation company. [SOURCE: Page_126]

Younes was highly appreciated by small farmers. He supplied good equipment, provided [SOURCE: Page_126]

credit facilities, and could give advice about the equipment. He managed to obtain the [SOURCE: Page_126]

official State license, and supplied a wide range of clients, including large-­‐scale farms. [SOURCE: Page_126]

Due to his extensive socio-­‐professional networks, he catered for increasingly complex [SOURCE: Page_126]

projects: 1) for state-­‐subsidized projects, he hired an engineer to design the project, as [SOURCE: Page_126]

the procedure requires a qualified engineer, 2) he provided the equipment for different [SOURCE: Page_126]

drip irrigation systems, generally a mixture of imported and Moroccan equipment, or [SOURCE: Page_126]

are made locally (by Mohamed, for example), and 3) he called on a local fitter to install [SOURCE: Page_126]

the equipment, when required. Drip irrigation systems thus belonged to multiple [SOURCE: Page_126]

(administrative, commercial, financial, technical) spheres, and they had to be adapted to [SOURCE: Page_126]

the myriad of specific requirements of different farmers. This encouraged the [SOURCE: Page_126]

emergence of actors like Younes, able to bridging these different spheres. This provided [SOURCE: Page_126]

business opportunities, but also recognition from some of the formal actors (state, [SOURCE: Page_126]

irrigation companies), who needed people like Younes to penetrate the market of small-­‐ scale farmers. [SOURCE: Page_126]

126 [SOURCE: Page_126]

3.3 Explaining the engagement of non-­‐conventional actors in diffusing drip [SOURCE: Page_127]

irrigation [SOURCE: Page_127]

We showed how four non-­‐conventional actors became involved in drip irrigation as [SOURCE: Page_127]

local artisans and traders, or as innovation intermediaries, and actively contributed to [SOURCE: Page_127]

adapting it to the requirements of a wide diversity of farmers, and to its rapid diffusion. [SOURCE: Page_127]

While doing so, their role and functions evolved depending on the dynamics of drip [SOURCE: Page_127]

irrigation and the socio-­‐technical networks, and their personal assessment of the [SOURCE: Page_127]

importance of drip irrigation for their professional career and social status. [SOURCE: Page_127]

We showed previously that there was a considerable mobility of innovation actors in [SOURCE: Page_127]

their professional activities, and in their role and functions in the socio-­‐technical [SOURCE: Page_127]

networks. Some actors even quit the drip irrigation sector. To explain this mobility, as [SOURCE: Page_127]

well as the capacity of actors to redeploy in other sectors, we analyze the two [SOURCE: Page_127]

dimensions of the resources that non-­‐conventional actors mobilize and produce in [SOURCE: Page_127]

interaction with the dynamics of the socio-­‐technical networks: 1) internal (cognitive) [SOURCE: Page_127]

resources versus external resources, and 2) social capital. Following Bourdieu, the [SOURCE: Page_127]

notion of social capital was extended to include the position these actors have in society, [SOURCE: Page_127]

which in the Moroccan context, means how actors relate to their environment (urban or [SOURCE: Page_127]

rural background). Intermediaries bring together knowledge from “disparate sources”, [SOURCE: Page_127]

and their background influences the “range of information that may be accessed” [SOURCE: Page_127]

(Nahapiet and Ghoshal 1998). Actors with an urban profile have easier access to formal [SOURCE: Page_127]

education; they can explore wider socio-­‐professional networks, and have different [SOURCE: Page_127]

personal and professional ambitions and reference models than those with a rural [SOURCE: Page_127]

background. It is argued that the interplay of these three dimensions (rural/urban [SOURCE: Page_127]

background, cognitive resources, social capital) help understand the role and functions [SOURCE: Page_127]

of innovation actors in the diffusion of drip irrigation, and on whether the actors remain [SOURCE: Page_127]

engaged in drip irrigation or redeploy in other professional activities. The two actors [SOURCE: Page_127]

who remained engaged in drip irrigation are first described, and then the two actors [SOURCE: Page_127]

who are no longer engaged. We scored each of the three dimensions, as well as the [SOURCE: Page_127]

ensuing capacity to re-­‐engage elsewhere (fig. 3). [SOURCE: Page_127]

Those who remain engaged in drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_127]

Driss had strong links to rurality (fig. 3) and always lived in a small village. His role [SOURCE: Page_127]

models were rural, and all related to the authority figures in the village (notables, big [SOURCE: Page_127]

farmers, local authorities) or to modern farmers who had a reputation for excelling in [SOURCE: Page_127]

agriculture. For Driss, drip irrigation was a way to be recognized as a modern farmer, [SOURCE: Page_127]

but also gave him social recognition as a drip specialist. During the three innovation [SOURCE: Page_127]

periods, Driss went to great trouble to improve his cognitive resources (knowledge, [SOURCE: Page_127]

know-­‐how) about drip irrigation. Driss also continuously invested in social capital, and [SOURCE: Page_127]

is part of a lively drip irrigation network. He trained fitters, who in turn consult him, and [SOURCE: Page_127]

keep him informed about what is going on in the surrounding villages. He remained in [SOURCE: Page_127]

touch with laborers on big farms so he could be regularly updated about new [SOURCE: Page_127]

agricultural/irrigation practices, and with drip artisans and traders for regular [SOURCE: Page_127]

127 [SOURCE: Page_127]

information about new equipment. He stuck to drip irrigation whatever it takes and his [SOURCE: Page_128]

capacity to re-­‐engage elsewhere appears to be limited, since he has remained in his rural [SOURCE: Page_128]

environment and has few external resources or opportunities. Going back to simply [SOURCE: Page_128]

being a farmer, even a modern one, would mean a loss of status. [SOURCE: Page_128]

Younes had an urban profile, although he had strong links with the countryside. He [SOURCE: Page_128]

started as a farm laborer, but lived and worked in a small town. His shop was [SOURCE: Page_128]

strategically located at a major intersection, and was an important local center for drip [SOURCE: Page_128]

irrigation. It was his social capital (being recognized as a person worthy of trust) more [SOURCE: Page_128]

than his cognitive resources that helped him during his drip irrigation career. During the [SOURCE: Page_128]

first period, he worked for a Spanish farmer, who installed drip irrigation 10 years [SOURCE: Page_128]

before anyone else. The farmer trusted him, let him handle the drip irrigation, and deal [SOURCE: Page_128]

with external actors (retailers, fitters). A few years before the second innovation period, [SOURCE: Page_128]

he became a fitter for a regional irrigation company, which dealt with a wide range of [SOURCE: Page_128]

often-­‐complex drip irrigation projects. This necessitated frequent interactions with [SOURCE: Page_128]

farmers, fitters, engineers, technicians, and even state officials for the subsidy [SOURCE: Page_128]

procedures. His experience in dealing with complex projects, and his relations with a [SOURCE: Page_128]

wide range of actors prompted a leading national irrigation company to ask him to [SOURCE: Page_128]

become their local representative. This relationship turned out to be very profitable [SOURCE: Page_128]

both for this company, which penetrated the local market of small-­‐scale farmers, and for [SOURCE: Page_128]

him as he became a licensed retailer. Younes has been successfully involved in drip [SOURCE: Page_128]

irrigation for more than 30 years; all his professional contacts are linked with this [SOURCE: Page_128]

innovation, and he is proud to be connected to all these different socio-­‐professional [SOURCE: Page_128]

spheres. He does not envisage leaving the drip irrigation sector, and his capacity to [SOURCE: Page_128]

reengage elsewhere appears limited. [SOURCE: Page_128]

Those who are no longer engaged in drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_128]

Aissam had a strong rural background; his professional ambitions remained linked to [SOURCE: Page_128]

rurality (fig.3). Aissam’s uncle, a big farmer, strongly influenced his professional career, [SOURCE: Page_128]

obtaining him jobs in the three successive innovation periods. Aissam was mainly [SOURCE: Page_128]

interested in making a living, something that drip irrigation provided to him for a while. [SOURCE: Page_128]

He was not able to capitalize on his knowledge and know-­‐how, and his cognitive [SOURCE: Page_128]

resources were limited. Although he was known in his village for a few years as a drip [SOURCE: Page_128]

irrigation fitter, even his social capital remained limited. He quickly lost his aura as a [SOURCE: Page_128]

specialist as drip irrigation users acquired the same know-­‐how and knowledge. In the [SOURCE: Page_128]

rapidly evolving world of drip irrigation, there was no further place for Aissam. [SOURCE: Page_128]

However, his uncle helped him find another job. [SOURCE: Page_128]

Mohamed had an urban profile; he was able to qualify as a mechanical technician. He [SOURCE: Page_128]

lived in Meknes, and had a workshop in a small town close to his (rural) clients. He was [SOURCE: Page_128]

often in Meknes, and compared his products with those of big regional companies, [SOURCE: Page_128]

staying informed about technological advances. Building on his initial training, [SOURCE: Page_128]

Mohamed took advantage of each innovation period to improve his cognitive resources. [SOURCE: Page_128]

128 [SOURCE: Page_128]

He invested considerably in his social capital. He trained young mechanics in his [SOURCE: Page_129]

workshop, and helped them set up their own workshops. He was recognized as the [SOURCE: Page_129]

“master” of his trade in the area, and was recently elected president of the local [SOURCE: Page_129]

association of mechanics. He was reputed to always be in advance of his peers. This also [SOURCE: Page_129]

explains why he (partially) moved out of drip irrigation, when the heydays of drip [SOURCE: Page_129]

irrigation were over. Indeed, thanks to his personal cognitive resources, his extensive [SOURCE: Page_129]

network, but also his professional reputation, he has proven his ability to re-­‐engage in [SOURCE: Page_129]

other fields of activity. [SOURCE: Page_129]

Figure 3 Intra and interpersonal attributes of four non-­‐conventional innovation actors [SOURCE: Page_129]

explaining their engagement in drip irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_129]

### 4. Discussion

4.1 Model of “interessement” versus model of diffusion [SOURCE: Page_130]

We analyzed an innovation in the making in a rural area in Morocco. It was shown how a [SOURCE: Page_130]

range of non-­‐conventional innovation intermediaries became involved in drip irrigation, [SOURCE: Page_130]

thereby progressively developing an active and inter-­‐related network for the sale, [SOURCE: Page_130]

manufacture, fitting, and use of drip irrigation systems. This network challenged an [SOURCE: Page_130]

imported technology aimed at large-­‐scale farmers and promoted by drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_130]

companies, and transformed it into three dynamic innovation systems, in which drip [SOURCE: Page_130]

irrigation systems were adapted to a wide range of situations, including small-­‐scale [SOURCE: Page_130]

farms. In line with Callon’s theory of translation, our analysis showed that “the network [SOURCE: Page_130]

dynamic may be considered as a process of extension which is based on the integration of [SOURCE: Page_130]

new technical characteristics, new objectives, and new actors” (Oerlemans and Assouline [SOURCE: Page_130]

2004). Drip irrigation companies initially viewed drip irrigation from a “diffusion [SOURCE: Page_130]

model” perspective, i.e. drip irrigation would spread to other farmers by “virtue of its [SOURCE: Page_130]

own qualities” and “via its demonstration” (Akrich, Callon, and Latour 2002). They [SOURCE: Page_130]

installed drip irrigation free of charge in some plots of large-­‐scale farmers, expecting [SOURCE: Page_130]

them to serve as a role model, and thereby create further demand and business for them. [SOURCE: Page_130]

We argue that in practice this model was rapidly replaced by a model of “interessement” [SOURCE: Page_130]

(Akrich, Callon, and Latour 2002), where the success of an innovation (its diffusion and [SOURCE: Page_130]

adoption) depends on the active participation of all those who are committed to [SOURCE: Page_130]

advancing its cause, in the process of which the technology is transformed. This is shown [SOURCE: Page_130]

by analyzing the four moments of translation in the innovation process (Callon 1986): 1) [SOURCE: Page_130]

problematization or how to become indispensable, 2) devices of ‘interessement’, 3) [SOURCE: Page_130]

enrolment of allies, and 4) mobilization of allies. [SOURCE: Page_130]

The ‘problematization’ happened initially when irrigation companies introduced drip [SOURCE: Page_130]

irrigation to selected large-­‐scale farmers, proclaiming the advantages of drip irrigation, [SOURCE: Page_130]

including water use efficiency and improved yields. These companies had the hardware, [SOURCE: Page_130]

mostly imported equipment for which they were official representatives, but also the [SOURCE: Page_130]

knowledge on how to install drip irrigation systems and to make them work, which, in [SOURCE: Page_130]

their view, made them “obligatory passage points” (Callon 1986). The ‘devices of [SOURCE: Page_130]

interessement’ they proposed consisted of equipping small plots free of charge for some [SOURCE: Page_130]

large-­‐scale farmers and enrolling them as clients. However, large-­‐scale farmers unlocked [SOURCE: Page_130]

the technology rapidly and turned to non-­‐conventional actors to make it work, to repair [SOURCE: Page_130]

the equipment, to manufacture certain components of the system, and to install it. Non-­‐ conventional actors were provided with many occasions for learning. They [SOURCE: Page_130]

progressively replaced the companies and constituted a dense network of local drip [SOURCE: Page_130]

irrigation intermediaries, catering to the new demand they were themselves creating. [SOURCE: Page_130]

During the second innovation period, local actors developed alternative drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_130]

systems for small-­‐scale horticultural farmers through a process they qualify as [SOURCE: Page_130]

“bricolage” (Benouniche, Zwarteveen, and Kuper 2014). The knowledge and know-­‐how [SOURCE: Page_130]

of such systems were distributed amongst a large number of actors, and the notion of [SOURCE: Page_130]

130 [SOURCE: Page_130]

becoming “indispensable” did not apply. Drip irrigation intermediaries installed [SOURCE: Page_131]

alternative systems in their own plots, or in those of relatives or friends, which thus [SOURCE: Page_131]

became ‘devices of interessement’. They proved that drip irrigation could work for [SOURCE: Page_131]

small-­‐scale farmers, and attracted their attention. This also increased their knowledge [SOURCE: Page_131]

and know-­‐how concerning drip irrigation, and established their credibility as drip [SOURCE: Page_131]

irrigation specialists. It provided the basis for enrolling small-­‐scale farmers in the drip [SOURCE: Page_131]

irrigation world, into which they themselves had been enrolled. We consider [SOURCE: Page_131]

‘enrolment’, which represents the “group of multilateral negotiations, trials of strength [SOURCE: Page_131]

and tricks that accompany the interessements and enable them to succeed” (Callon 1986) [SOURCE: Page_131]

as a dynamic process through which actors join the process of development, adaptation, [SOURCE: Page_131]

fabrication, diffusion, and use of an innovation, thereby accelerating its adoption. New [SOURCE: Page_131]

trades emerged, and a set of interrelated roles was defined, in which local drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_131]

intermediaries (retailers, manufacturers, fitters) interacted with large and small-­‐scale [SOURCE: Page_131]

farmers, as well as with drip irrigation technology. [SOURCE: Page_131]

Interestingly, during the third innovation period the drip irrigation companies staged a [SOURCE: Page_131]

comeback by creating working relations with non-­‐conventional actors, who had [SOURCE: Page_131]

acquired sufficient legitimacy to become Callon’s “obligatory passage points”. [SOURCE: Page_131]

Intermediaries were familiar with local conditions and local users while drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_131]

companies were a source of information about new technologies, and subsidy [SOURCE: Page_131]

procedures. These actors mobilized each other in their quest to extend their business. [SOURCE: Page_131]

They became allies by the reciprocal acceptance of the other being an indispensable [SOURCE: Page_131]

spokesman for large and small-­‐scale farmers on the one hand, and the international [SOURCE: Page_131]

manufacturers as well as the State, on the other (Akrich, Callon, and Latour 2002). In a [SOURCE: Page_131]

way, both the State and the irrigation companies thus validated the second order [SOURCE: Page_131]

innovations already experimented in the field, and which had become a credible [SOURCE: Page_131]

alternative to imported equipment (Knickel et al. 2009). [SOURCE: Page_131]

4.2 Change and be changed: interplay between non-­‐conventional innovation [SOURCE: Page_131]

actors and drip irrigation technology [SOURCE: Page_131]

Drip irrigation companies initially viewed the attributes of drip irrigation essentially in [SOURCE: Page_131]

technical terms. The technology would save water, and allow agricultural intensification. [SOURCE: Page_131]

These attributes were marketing arguments to attract large-­‐scale farmers, who became [SOURCE: Page_131]

their clients. Interestingly, this discourse was later appropriated by the State, which [SOURCE: Page_131]

started subsidy programs for drip irrigation. However, for (future) drip irrigation users, [SOURCE: Page_131]

local artisans and traders, and innovation intermediaries, the technology also seduced [SOURCE: Page_131]

through additional attributes such as the “modernity” associated with those involved in [SOURCE: Page_131]

drip irrigation, the rapid diffusion of drip irrigation creating job opportunities, the [SOURCE: Page_131]

technicality of drip irrigation appealing to young farmers and local technicians alike, or [SOURCE: Page_131]

the possibility to connect through drip irrigation to other socio-­‐professional spheres, [SOURCE: Page_131]

which would otherwise remain closed to such actors. These different attributes of drip [SOURCE: Page_131]

irrigation led the innovation actors to engage with drip irrigation. We argue that their [SOURCE: Page_131]

131 [SOURCE: Page_131]

commitment induced reciprocal changes in the technology and in their socio-­‐ professional status. [SOURCE: Page_132]

Drip irrigation technology adapted by non-­‐conventional innovation actors [SOURCE: Page_132]

It was shown how drip irrigation technology was taken out of the hands of the drip [SOURCE: Page_132]

irrigation companies by innovation actors, who adapted the technology to their world: [SOURCE: Page_132]

the small plots of small-­‐scale farmers, or the large plots operated by not always qualified [SOURCE: Page_132]

laborers. Akrich (1998) showed how users introduce modifications, and change the [SOURCE: Page_132]

technology or even the meaning of the innovation as defined by the original designers. [SOURCE: Page_132]

During the innovation process, both the technology and the environment in which it is [SOURCE: Page_132]

developed and used are no longer controlled by the actor who designed the technology [SOURCE: Page_132]

(Beguin 2007). [SOURCE: Page_132]

Dynamic alliances between drip irrigation and non-­‐conventional innovation actors [SOURCE: Page_132]

It was shown how non-­‐conventional innovation actors integrated the drip irrigation [SOURCE: Page_132]

world, attracted by specific attributes of the technology, thereby transforming it. But [SOURCE: Page_132]

they also changed themselves, and their socio-­‐professional status evolved. We argue that [SOURCE: Page_132]

the changes in the socio-­‐professional status particularly concerned the innovation actors [SOURCE: Page_132]

who introduced changes in the technology or in the institutions related to drip [SOURCE: Page_132]

irrigation, or rather those actors who had the capacity to continually operate changes. [SOURCE: Page_132]

During the first period, actors accumulated knowledge and know-­‐how. A local welder, [SOURCE: Page_132]

for instance, purchased an expensive imported filter system and dismantled it in order [SOURCE: Page_132]

to be able to manufacture his own. In the second innovation period, actors improved [SOURCE: Page_132]

their knowledge and know-­‐how by mobilizing materiel objects. In our case, this related [SOURCE: Page_132]

to farm plots, which enabled innovation intermediaries to improve their knowledge and [SOURCE: Page_132]

know-­‐how, as they installed, adapted and used small-­‐scale drip irrigation systems. [SOURCE: Page_132]

Intermediaries who did not have their own farm plots were obliged to install the drip [SOURCE: Page_132]

irrigation system directly on plots of customers. They were not entitled to mistakes, and [SOURCE: Page_132]

could not consolidate their learning, and were subsequently excluded from the [SOURCE: Page_132]

innovation process. During the third period, the formal actors (the State, national [SOURCE: Page_132]

irrigation companies) increasingly recognized the knowledge and know-­‐how of local [SOURCE: Page_132]

innovation actors. A local actor, with no academic qualification, thus progressed from [SOURCE: Page_132]

farm laborer to fitter, and then to retailer. He even obtained a license, as a local [SOURCE: Page_132]

representative, from a national irrigation company. In his view, this license is certainly [SOURCE: Page_132]

an institutional recognition, albeit not an academic one. [SOURCE: Page_132]

Social capital played an important role in determining the pathways of the non-­‐ conventional innovation actors and their socio-­‐professional status. We opted for [SOURCE: Page_132]

Bourdieu’s viewpoint, whose definition of social capital 1) refers to the network of [SOURCE: Page_132]

connections a person can effectively mobilize, and 2) depends on real practices of [SOURCE: Page_132]

communication, and is based on mutual cognition and recognition (Siisiäinen 2003). In [SOURCE: Page_132]

other words, the knowledge and know-­‐how of innovation intermediaries only exist in [SOURCE: Page_132]

the ‘eyes of the others’. This is how a local retailer who continuously invested in [SOURCE: Page_132]

132 [SOURCE: Page_132]

developing his business reputation became a major player in drip irrigation in our study [SOURCE: Page_133]

area. De Carolis and Saparito (2006) consider that social capital can facilitate access to [SOURCE: Page_133]

information, enhancing the timing, relevance, and quality of information. This explains [SOURCE: Page_133]

why some people and not others exploit opportunities. In our case, the innovation [SOURCE: Page_133]

intermediaries invested considerably in developing networks that would keep them [SOURCE: Page_133]

informed on new practices, technologies, and institutions. [SOURCE: Page_133]

Conclusion [SOURCE: Page_133]

It was shown how an innovation seduced and enrolled non-­‐conventional actors in the [SOURCE: Page_133]

process of diffusion of innovation. We conclude that the diffusion of an innovation does [SOURCE: Page_133]

not rely solely on the attributes envisaged by the original designers and researchers. In [SOURCE: Page_133]

actual use, the innovation may have other attributes that explain its adoption by actors, [SOURCE: Page_133]

but also the enrolment of innovation intermediaries and local artisans and traders. In [SOURCE: Page_133]

the case of drip irrigation, the aura of modernity, and opportunities for professional [SOURCE: Page_133]

promotion or recognition, enrolled these non-­‐conventional innovation actors. They [SOURCE: Page_133]

became objective allies who provided local support to drip irrigation users, accelerating [SOURCE: Page_133]

the diffusion of the innovation. This study contributed to the debate on understanding [SOURCE: Page_133]

the individual motivations of non-­‐conventional innovation actors in agricultural [SOURCE: Page_133]

innovation systems. Understanding these motivations is important to apprehend the [SOURCE: Page_133]

multiple pathways of the innovation process, and of the innovation actors. In a next step, [SOURCE: Page_133]

it would be interesting to analyze the dynamic interactions between these different non-­‐ conventional innovation actors, as they change not only the technology, but also change [SOURCE: Page_133]

their own socio-­‐professional status and that of other innovation actors through [SOURCE: Page_133]

extended socio-­‐technical networks. From an exclusive imported technology concerning [SOURCE: Page_133]

only a small minority of large-­‐scale farmers, drip irrigation became an inclusive [SOURCE: Page_133]

innovation, accessible to a wide range of actors. The diffusion of drip irrigation is thus [SOURCE: Page_133]

explained by its power of seduction for local actors. Once seduced, these actors move up [SOURCE: Page_133]

the social ladder, and become not only vectors of innovation, but also the creators of [SOURCE: Page_133]

new technical professions. The successful diffusion of drip simultaneously ensures their [SOURCE: Page_133]

social and professional advancement, and they in turn seduce others as they go. It is [SOURCE: Page_133]

worth considering integrating these actors in state programs and other planned [SOURCE: Page_133]

innovation processes, such as the large-­‐scale, often State-­‐led, on-­‐going irrigation [SOURCE: Page_133]

projects in many countries around the world. Often considered ‘unprofessional’ or even [SOURCE: Page_133]

impostors, we conclude that non-­‐conventional innovation should be considered as allies [SOURCE: Page_133]

in such innovation processes. They are near to field realities and to the users of the [SOURCE: Page_133]

innovation. They are also able to adapt a technology while accounting to local [SOURCE: Page_133]

requirements. Considering them allies will motivate such actors, often interested in [SOURCE: Page_133]

institutional recognition, to be active in the field. [SOURCE: Page_133]
